A test runs every account function as a role made by `grantWriterRole`, shows `checkWriterConnection` accepts it and refuses one more privilege on any account table, and shows it can't delete accounts, truncate counters or disable triggers. T-032's per-grant test now covers these tables too.
- **Guard triggers fire always** (D-067's rule, 0008 part 4): 0009 sets `ENABLE ALWAYS` on every trigger it makes, so a session in replica mode can't skip them (tested, with `tgenabled = 'A'` checked). Tests that age an invitation turn its identity trigger off inside their own transaction and back on `ALWAYS` before it commits.
- **Fixed by the database, for every writer.** A user's `email` can't be changed by UPDATE: there is no supported way to change a sign-in address yet (D-052 wants the password and the second factor again, so it waits for T-037/T-038, and its own migration will relax the identity trigger), and an address must not drift from the invitations that made the account and its memberships. `system_admin` can't change either, and `CHECK (system_admin = bootstrap)` means the bootstrap account is the only system admin: making and removing system admins is T-041/T-048's, which will need a sound rule (who may promote, at least one active admin left) and a migration replacing this check. *Rejected for now:* a trigger allowing `system_admin = true` only "when no active admin exists" (a writer could disable every admin first, so it guards nothing).
- **What the writer can still do with direct SQL** (beside D-067's list), precisely:
  - **mint an account, or add any existing user to any organization**, but only one way: insert a *pending* invitation, dated now and naming an active system admin as inviter (any admin's ID, which it can read), with a token hash of its choosing; accept it by UPDATE now; then insert the user (for that invitation's address) and the membership, or, for an address that already has an account, just the membership of that user in that organization: the triggers then allow either. It can't insert an invitation already accepted or revoked, back- or forward-dated, expiring in under an hour or over 14 days, or from a user who isn't an active system admin (`invitations_check_new`), nor accept one that expired or date an acceptance or revocation other than now, nor revoke as a non-admin (`invitations_settle_once`). Tested as the writer role, including that the one remaining sequence works;
  - set any user's `password_hash` to a hash of a password it knows (so take over an account, short of two-step sign-in once T-038 exists), with the one allowed setting `password_hash_ok` accepts;
  - set or clear `disabled_at`, change `display_name` and `last_sign_in_at`;
  - delete or rewrite throttle counters (lifting a wait or keeping someone waiting);
  - revoke any pending invitation in an active admin's name;
  - read every password hash and invitation token hash (SELECT on every table).

  **What it can't do:** make an account without an accepted invitation (other than the one bootstrap row), add a membership without an accepted invitation of that organization for that user's address (a forged pending one, accepted by UPDATE now, is that; see above), change a user's address or system admin flag, make a second system admin, change a settled invitation, or move an invitation to another organization or address. All of this holds in replica mode too (tested). Column grants would narrow UPDATE on `users`, but D-067's allowlist refuses column privileges, so the writer role's password is as sensitive as D-067 says.
- **Review fixes (independent review of `cfa02da`).**
  - *The inviter can't learn whether an address has an account.* An inviter holding the link could otherwise open it and send a bad name: an existing account answered `account_exists`, a new address `invalid_display_name`. Two changes: `createInvitation` never returns the token; it hands it only to a `deliver` function (an `InvitationDelivery`; when it is called and what a failed send does are in the second review fixes below), which T-040 makes send the email. And `acceptInvitation` checks the whole form (name and password) before it reads anything about the address, so an incomplete form gets the same answer either way (tested for five bad forms). Only someone with the invitee's mailbox can send a complete form and learn `account_exists`, and that is the invitee. *Rejected:* answering "accepted, sign in to finish" for an existing account (it would claim the invitation for whoever holds the link, and the next step still differs).
  - *Failures cost the same for any stored hash.* A wrong password for a hash with cheaper settings than today's (an old row) is padded with one dummy run at today's settings, so it no longer fails in a few milliseconds. A correct password for an old hash is then re-hashed, which costs a full run anyway.
  - *Stored settings that throw or cost seconds are never run.* Replaced in the second review (below) by a fixed allowed set.
  - *Passwords in NFC.* Every password is put in Unicode NFC (`passwordText`) before its length is measured, before the common-password and context-word checks, and before it is hashed or verified, so "é" typed precomposed or as "e" plus a combining accent is one password. This replaces D-052's "no normalization"; NFC, not NFKC, so compatibility characters (full-width letters) stay distinct. A lone surrogate (not well-formed UTF-16, with no UTF-8 form) is refused: `invalid_text` for a new password, `invalid_request` at sign-in, and `verifyPassword` treats it as a dummy-hashed failure. Nothing is deployed, so no stored hash needs moving.
  - *No raw database errors.* At sign-in, an address containing NUL (which PostgreSQL text can't hold) or a lone surrogate, or such a password, is `invalid_request` before anything is read, counted or hashed. That is not counted against the throttle: it says nothing about any account, and a form never sends it. Everywhere else NUL is already refused by `normalizeEmail` and `validateDisplayName`, and `clearSignInThrottle` only hashes what it is given. Invitations for one organization and address are serialised by `pg_advisory_xact_lock(68, hashtext(organization || ' ' || address))`, so a second waits and revokes the first instead of failing the unique index (12 at once on two connections, tested). The first sign-in's dummy hash is inside the `busy` handling, so a full queue answers `busy` rather than throwing (tested in its own file with no database).
- **For T-037 and T-045: what the client address counter can and can't tell apart.** The source key is only as good as the address the HTTP layer gives `signIn`:
  - `::1` and other loopback addresses, IPv4-compatible forms (`::192.0.2.1`, which counts as the IPv6 /64 `0:0:0:0::/64`, shared by every such address), NAT64 (`64:ff9b::/96`, so every IPv4 client behind one NAT64 gateway shares one /64 counter), and everything unreadable (`source:other`) each share one bucket.
  - Behind a proxy that isn't configured as trusted (D-051, D-058), or on a Unix socket, every request has the proxy's or no address, so **everyone shares one counter**: 20 failures by anyone make everyone wait. T-045 (or T-037, whichever starts the sign-in route) must refuse to start, or at least warn loudly, when the client address can't be read or is the same for every request.
  - Rows grow with the address space used: an attacker with an IPv6 /48 has 65,536 /64s, so up to that many source counters (and 20 free guesses each across all accounts, still bounded per account by the account counter) until `deleteExpiredSignInThrottles` runs. T-037's cleanup command must run it on a schedule.
- **Second review fixes (review of `78c5911`).**
  - *Invitations are committed before they are sent.* `createInvitation` stores the invitation (pending, token hash only) and commits, so the advisory lock and the connection are released, and only then calls `deliver(message, signal)` outside any transaction, with a time limit (`deliveryTimeoutMs`, default 30 s, at most 5 minutes) after which `signal` aborts and the send is abandoned. If the send throws, rejects or times out, a second short transaction revokes the invitation in the inviter's name (`invitations_settle_once` allows an inviter to revoke their own invitation even if disabled meanwhile) and the reply is `delivery_failed`. If it was accepted meanwhile (the message did get through), it is left alone and the reply is a success. A failed revocation still answers `delivery_failed`; the link was never shown here and still expires. The token is never in any reply. A database error from the first transaction is a fixed `database_unavailable` (never the database's message); the inviter losing system admin between the check and the INSERT, which the insert trigger then refuses, is `not_permitted`. Tests: a send held open while the pool's only connection serves other queries and another invitation for the address goes through; a timeout that aborts and revokes; a failure that revokes; an acceptance during a "failed" send left in place; concurrent creation still leaves one pending. *Rejected:* an outbox table (a row per message, sent by a worker) until T-040 needs retries; if one is added, it keeps the token only until sent.
  - *A lost connection can't crash the process* (pre-existing in `client.ts`). A checked-out client had no `'error'` listener, so a connection the server ended while a transaction awaited something else (a timer, a send) raised an uncaught exception. `transaction` now listens for the whole checkout, removes the listener at release, releases the client as broken so the pool discards it, and fails with `ConnectionLostError` (code `08006`, fixed message, the driver's error as `cause`). Tested with `pg_terminate_backend` from another connection while the transaction waits on a timer; the test fails without the listener.
  - *Only allowed hash settings are run.* `ALLOWED_SCRYPT_SETTINGS` is today's settings only (ln=14, r=8, p=5), and `password_hash_ok` accepts exactly those. A stored hash with any other settings is treated like an unknown user's: never run, one dummy run at today's settings, refused (so it can't throw, cost seconds or have a time of its own; tested for timing parity). **The rule for a settings change** (U-004's Argon2id included): the new decision entry adds the new settings, keeps the previous ones in `ALLOWED_SCRYPT_SETTINGS` and `password_hash_ok` only until a planned forced re-hash or reset (people who haven't signed in by then reset their password), and pads failures against a cheaper previous setting to today's cost. A failure against a costlier previous setting is slower than today's during that window, so whether an account still has the old hash can be told from timing; that is accepted for the window and must be noted in that entry.
  - *Context words in NFC.* `passwordContextWords` puts names and addresses in NFC and keeps combining marks inside words (`\p{M}`), so a name typed as "Amélie" or "Amélie" gives the same context word as the (NFC) password is compared with.
- **Binding rule for T-040, T-045 and anything after: invitation tokens are secret to the invitee.** A token appears only in the message `deliver` sends to the invitation's address. It never appears in logs (request, error or mail logs), development or test mail transports that print or store messages where others can read them, API responses, the admin's screens, or any "copy invite link" button. An outbox, if one is ever added, keeps the token only until the message is sent and then drops it. Reset tokens (T-039) follow the same rule.
- Sources: OWASP ASVS 5.0 V6 (6.1.1, 6.2.1–6.2.12, 6.3.1, 6.3.8, 6.5.2) and V7.2.3; OWASP Authentication, Password Storage and Forgot Password cheat sheets (account lockout, device cookies); Node.js `crypto.scrypt` docs (`maxmem`); RFC 5321 (address lengths), RFC 4648 (base64url), RFC 5737/RFC 3849/RFC 1918 (example addresses in tests); SecLists `Passwords/Common-Credentials/10k-most-common.txt` and its MIT license (github.com/danielmiessler/SecLists), fetched 2026-11-23.
- **Amendment (2026-11-23, autopilot S-016, from the third T-036 review).** `createInvitation`'s answers, as its doc comment lists them:
  - *Superseded is not a success.* If a newer invitation to the same organization and address revoked this one while its message was being sent (the newer one revokes and inserts in one transaction, so its `created_at` equals this one's `revoked_at`), the reply is the refusal `superseded`, with no ID: only the newer invitation works, and an ID that `revokeInvitation` would call unknown is useless to the inviter. One an admin withdrew during the send is `revoked`. `ok` now means the invitation was stored, sent and is still pending (or was accepted already). If that state can't be read after a successful send, the reply is `ok` (it was stored and sent). *Rejected:* `ok` with a `state` field (a caller checking only `ok` would keep the dead ID, which is the bug).
  - *A failed withdrawal is said.* When the send fails, the revocation is tried twice; if both fail with a database or network failure, the reply is `delivery_failed_may_be_pending` ("may still be pending until it expires; send a new one, which replaces it, or withdraw it"), never "withdrawn". The link was still never shown to anyone.
  - *Only real database or network failures are `database_unavailable`.* `isDatabaseFailure` (client.ts) is true for a server error whose SQLSTATE class means the connection or the server failed (08 connection, 40 transaction rollback, 53 resources, 57 operator intervention, 58 system, XX internal), `ConnectionLostError`, a Node system error from the socket or name lookup (an `E…` code, underscores allowed as in `EAI_AGAIN`, never `ERR_*`, with a `syscall`), an `AggregateError` from Node's dual-stack connect (its `code` is such a code, or all its errors are failures) and pg's own code-less connection errors (a fixed list of its messages). Everything else is rethrown: syntax, permission, data and constraint errors (42, 22, 23 …), Node's `ERR_*` codes and TypeErrors. A programming error must fail loudly, not look like an outage. The same rule decides whether a failed withdrawal is retried. `createInvitation`'s known database refusals are mapped before it (`invitations_invited_by_admin` to `not_permitted`), and the advisory lock keeps two invitations for one address from racing on the unique index.
  - `createDatabase`/`dropDatabase`'s bare `pg.Client` now has an `'error'` listener, so the server ending that connection rejects the query instead of crashing the process.
- **For T-045 (and anything that logs an error's `cause` or fields): pg's `where` can carry bound values.** The driver copies the server's CONTEXT into `error.where`. When `log_parameter_max_length_on_error` is above 0 (the default is 0; a host, a role or a session can raise it), CONTEXT includes `unnamed portal with parameters: $1 = '…', $2 = '…'`, the query's bound values (trimmed to that many bytes), which here can be addresses, password hashes, invitation token hashes or record text. Checked on PostgreSQL 16. So a server log must not write `where` (or the whole error object) as is: log the SQLSTATE, constraint and message, drop or redact `where`, or check that `log_parameter_max_length_on_error` is 0 at startup. The same holds for `detail` of unique violations, which shows the conflicting key. Source: https://www.postgresql.org/docs/16/runtime-config-logging.html (`log_parameter_max_length_on_error`), read 2026-11-23.
- **John (2026-11-23): keep the wait.** Waits after wrong guesses apply to every device; no remembered-device bypass. Someone guessing a few times an hour can keep a person waiting up to 15 minutes; an admin can clear the counter, and two-step sign-in means guessing the password alone never gets anyone in.
- **Amendment (2026-11-24, manual session, S-018): known addresses, and a re-entry counter of its own.** The 2026-11-24 security review showed the wait above is not "up to 15 minutes": an attempt during a wait isn't counted, but one wrong guess let through each time the wait ends restarts it, so a stranger guessing about 4 times an hour from anywhere keeps the right password answering `throttled` for as long as they like. For the install's only system admin there was no way back (`clearSignInThrottle` needs an active system admin), and `reauthenticate` shared the counter, so the same stranger could also hold a signed-in person off every sensitive change. Fixed in `packages/core/src/sign-in-throttle.ts` (`decideSignInAttempt`), `packages/db/src/accounts.ts` and `0016_sign_in_known_sources.sql` (0015 is T-039's):
  - **A known address.** A client address key (`throttleSourceKey`; never `source:other`, which everyone unreadable shares) from which the account signed in with the right password in the last 30 days (`KNOWN_SOURCE_SECONDS`) is *known* for that account: a row of `sign_in_known_sources (account_key, source_key, signed_in_at, failures, last_failure_at)`, keyed like the counters (a hash of the address typed, so no address is stored). Each known address has **its own counter for that account, with the account rule** (5 free, then 1 s doubling to 15 minutes, forgotten after a day). An attempt from a known address goes ahead if the address counter allows it **and either** the account counter or its own counter does; so during an account wait the password is still checked there. Every attempt let through is counted up front on every counter that applies (the account counter too, even during its wait, since the password is then checked), and a refusal counts nothing. A right password clears the account counter (as before) and the known address's counter, and makes the address known (or renews it). Rows are read and locked in the same transaction as the two counters, after them (lock order: counters in key order, then the known row), and the cleanup deletes known rows 30 days after their last right password.
  - **What it gives.** A stranger anywhere but the person's own recent networks can no longer keep them out there; at a known address the person (or anyone sharing that address) still gets at most the account rule's guesses (about 100 a day), on top of the account counter's. An unknown address still waits for the account counter exactly as before, so **waits after wrong guesses still apply on every device and network the account hasn't recently signed in from** (John, 2026-11-23, above). This is not the remembered-device bypass John declined: no cookie or device token, nothing that skips counting; a known address is throttled by a counter of its own that strangers elsewhere can't move. The reply to a stranger is unchanged whether or not the address typed has known addresses (the known row is read the same way for any address typed, and only a right password ever makes one; tested).
  - **Why this and not the alternatives** (from the S-018 entry): checking the password during every wait would leave the account's guess budget bounded only per client address (an IPv6 /48 has 65,536 of them); a lower cap still lets a stranger hold the account for ever, only with more guesses; counting per (account, address) pair alone loses the account-wide bound. Two-step sign-in (D-052, D-077) means a password guessed at a known address still gets no one in.
  - **Residual.** Someone on the person's own network (the same IPv4 address behind one NAT, or the same /64) can still push that address's counter, and a person at a new network (travelling, a new office) still waits behind a stranger's guesses until an admin clears the counter (`clearSignInThrottle`) or they sign in from a known address. The last way back when the only system admin is held at a new network: the database owner deletes the counter, `DELETE FROM public.sign_in_throttles WHERE key = 'account:' || encode(sha256(convert_to(lower('<the address>'), 'UTF8')), 'hex');` (the same key core's `throttleAccountKey` makes), with no restart needed. Creating a second system admin is left to T-041/T-048 (D-077's note on `system_admin`).
  - **John (2026-11-24): approved.** An address that signed in successfully in the last 30 days may still try the password during an account wait; strangers keep waiting.
  - **Review fixes (2026-11-24).** *At most 20 known addresses per account* (`MAX_KNOWN_SOURCES`): each right password keeps the 20 newest by last sign-in and deletes the rest, in the same transaction (after the account counter's row, so in the usual lock order); tested with 25 old rows. *Accepted signal on a shared network:* during an account wait, someone on a network the account signed in from (the same IPv4 address or /64) gets the password checked for a real address (`invalid_credentials`) while a made-up one answers `throttled`, so neighbours on that network can learn that an account exists and has signed in there. No cheap fix keeps the person's way in (made-up known rows for every typed address would let anyone fill the table); the people who learn it already share the person's network, and two-step sign-in still stands between them and the account.
  - **Re-entry has its own counter.** `reauthenticate` (D-077) now counts wrong passwords on `reauth:<user ID>` (core's `throttleReauthKey`, the account rule) and the address counter, never on the account counter typed at sign-in, and doesn't use or record known addresses. Only someone holding one of the person's sessions can move it, and strangers' guesses at the sign-in form no longer stop a signed-in person from re-entering. The `sign_in_throttles` key check allows `reauth:` keys (and keeps 0015's `reset:` keys, T-039, D-078).
  - **Writer grants:** `sign_in_known_sources` INSERT, UPDATE (also for its `FOR UPDATE`), DELETE (cleanup), in `ACCOUNT_TABLE_PRIVILEGES`. The writer can insert a known row for any account and address; it can already set any password hash (above), so this adds nothing it couldn't do.
  - Tests (`accounts.test.ts`, `second-factor.test.ts`, core `sign-in-throttle.test.ts`): the review's repro (a stranger guessing once per 15-minute wait, three rounds) with the only system admin getting in from their own address each round while a new address still waits 900 s; a known address getting five checks then its own wait; the address counter still applying at a known address; a known address forgotten after 30 days and deleted by the cleanup; `source:other` and a wrong password never making one; identical replies to a stranger for an address with and without known addresses; and re-entry working while strangers hold the sign-in counter, with its own wrong passwords counted on `reauth:` only.

## D-069 — Sessions: a hashed token per sign-in, a second-step stage that grants nothing, limits the database enforces, one organization per session (2026-11-23, autopilot T-037)
Builds D-052's session part on D-068's sign-in. Pure rules in `packages/core/src/sessions.ts`; data access in `packages/db/src/sessions.ts` and `second-step.ts`; the table in `packages/db/migrations/0010_sessions.sql`; the cleanup command in `packages/db/src/cleanup-cli.ts` (`npm run db:cleanup`). No new dependency (`node:crypto` only). Checked against OWASP ASVS 5.0 V7 (7.1–7.5) and V3.3.
- **The token** is 32 bytes from `randomBytes` as base64url without padding: 43 characters, **one spelling** (`isSessionToken` refuses padding, the standard alphabet, spaces, a wrong length, and a last character with stray low bits, which Node would decode to the same bytes). A malformed token is refused before the database is asked. Only its SHA-256 is stored (`token_hash`, unique, 32 bytes); a plain hash suffices at 256 bits (as D-068's invitations). Lookup is by that hash through the unique index, so no stored secret is ever compared in code; the CSRF check, which does compare, uses `timingSafeEqual`.
- **Two stages, so a password alone never gives a full session (D-068).** `beginSession` runs `signIn` (throttle, dummy hash and all) and stores a session in stage `second_step`: it lasts exactly 10 minutes from the password (a CHECK), and it can only be read (`readPendingSession`, for T-038's code screen), ended (`endSession`) or promoted. `resolveSession`, `rotateSession`, `switchOrganization`, `reauthenticate`, `listSessions` and the ending functions all refuse it. `promoteSession` moves it to `full` with a **new token**, and needs a `SecondStepProof`: an object made by `passedSecondStep(sessionId, userId)` in `second-step.ts`, recorded in a module-private WeakMap (a look-alike object isn't in it), for that session and user, at most 60 seconds old, spent on success (two promotions with one proof on two connections: one wins, tested). **Until T-038, nothing in the product calls `passedSecondStep`, so no session can become full.** Neither function is exported from `@oculus/db`, and a test fails if any source file under `packages/*/src`, `scripts/` or `tools/` other than `sessions.ts`, `second-step.ts` and the session tests mentions `second-step`, `passedSecondStep` or `promoteSession`. That test is a speed bump that catches an accidental import; it can't see a renamed re-export or a dynamic import, and **code review is the real control**. **T-038 must** call `passedSecondStep` only after checking a code for that pending session's own user, add its verifier to that test's list in the same change, extend `reauthenticate` to ask for the second step too (D-052), and in its migration tie promotion to its own record of the passed step, so a writer with direct SQL can't promote by UPDATE (below).
- **Organization (D-019).** A session works in one organization. It is chosen at the password (`organizationId`, which `signIn` checks) or at promotion: the one given, else the user's only membership, else `choose_organization` with the user's own organizations, or `organization_required` for a system admin with no memberships (the proof is kept either way, so the person needn't type a new code). A system admin may work in any organization that exists. A sign-in naming an organization the person can't enter is `invalid_credentials` and stays counted as a failed attempt; for a system admin that means one that doesn't exist (`signIn` now checks it exists, review item 7), so naming a made-up organization can't be used to clear the account's wait. **Switching** (`switchOrganization`) makes a new session row with a new token for the same sign-in (same `signed_in_at`, second step and absolute end; the re-entered-password time is not carried over) and deletes the old one in one transaction; an organization the person can't work in and one that doesn't exist are the same `not_permitted`. The database refuses any other change of `organization_id`.
- **Time limits, enforced by the database's clock in the query itself.** Every lookup is one statement (`UPDATE … SET last_seen_at = clock_timestamp() … WHERE token_hash = $1 AND stage = 'full' AND session_live(s) AND session_access_ok(user, organization, signed_in_at) RETURNING …`): `expires_at` (absolute, `signed_in_at + absolute_seconds`, a CHECK) and `last_seen_at + idle_seconds`, an active account, a sign-in after the account's `sessions_valid_after`, and a membership of the session's organization or system admin. Defaults 4 hours idle and 12 hours absolute (D-052, John); `beginSession`'s `policy` may set idle 5 minutes to 12 hours and absolute 1 to 24 hours, idle ≤ absolute (`SESSION_POLICY_BOUNDS`, and the table's CHECKs say the same). Each session keeps the limits it was made with; T-048's System settings pass the chosen policy in. As ASVS 7.1.1 asks, these are the documented limits. An expired session is refused whether or not the cleanup runs.
- **Rotation (ASVS 7.2.4):** a new token at sign-in, at promotion (the database refuses a promotion that keeps the token), at a change of organization, at re-entering the password (review item 3; the database refuses a new `password_at` without a new token), and on `rotateSession`, which T-019d (site unlocks) and T-041 (role changes) must call. The old token stops at once (tested across two connections). The CSRF token follows.
- **CSRF (D-052):** `csrfTokenFor(sessionToken)` is HMAC-SHA-256 keyed by the session token's bytes over a fixed label: no storage, it changes with every rotation, and it can't be worked back to the session token. The admin fetches it with a same-origin request and sends it on every change; `checkCsrfToken` compares in constant time. With D-058's Origin check this is the protection T-042 wires into every change route. *Rejected:* a stored random CSRF token (another secret column, rotated separately); signing with a server key (`OCULUS_SECRET_KEY` is for TOTP secrets, and losing it would then sign everyone out).
- **Re-entering the password** (`reauthenticate`, ASVS 7.5.1): the session's own user's address and the typed password go through `signIn`, so the same throttle counts every guess and the same refusals come back. Success stamps `password_at` and gives the session a **new token** (the caller writes the new cookie; the old token stops); `resolveSession` reports `recentlyAuthenticated` (10 minutes by default, 1 to 60 minutes if asked). Sensitive changes (T-039, T-048, T-019d's site password) must check it. Sign-in itself counts, so a fresh session is recent.
- **A person's own sessions (ASVS 7.5.2, 7.4.3):** `listSessions` returns only the signed-in person's live sessions (both stages, every organization, newest use first, at most 200), with ID, organization, stage, times, a label and which one is current: never a token, a hash or a client address. `endOwnSession` ends one by ID; another person's ID, an unknown one and a malformed one are the same `unknown_session`. `endOtherSessions` is "sign out everywhere else". `endSession` is sign-out, for either stage. `endUserSessions(db, adminToken, userId)` is a system admin's (ASVS 7.4.5): it takes the admin's own session token and checks `systemAdmin` from that session in the same transaction, never a caller-supplied user ID (review item 2), and needs the admin's password entered in the last 10 minutes (`reauthentication_required`, second review item 4). It reads the admin's session without locking or refreshing it, so the only row lock it takes is the target user row's (second review item 1: locking the admin's session row first deadlocked with `endOtherSessions` and switching on the same account; tested with PostgreSQL's deadlock counter). `endAllSessionsForUser(tx, userId)` (not exported from the index) is for T-039's reset inside its own transaction; it checks no permission. The label comes from the caller (core's `clientLabel`: 200 code points, no control or direction characters); no address is stored with a session.
- **Ending sessions is a time as well as a DELETE, and can't race making them (independent review of fbb42c1, B1).** The review found that under Read Committed a `DELETE … WHERE user_id = X` never sees a row another transaction inserts at the same moment: a session copied by `switchOrganization` (or a waiting session from `beginSession`) survived "sign out everywhere else" 28 times in 30, an admin's "end all" 24 in 30, and a disable 33 in 40, reviving on re-enable. Two fixes, each enough for the cases it covers:
  - *A per-user time.* `users.sessions_valid_after` (0010, default `-infinity`): a session whose `signed_in_at` (which a switched copy keeps) is not after it is dead in every lookup, at insert and at promotion (`session_access_ok`'s third argument). Disabling an account or changing its system admin flag moves it to now in a BEFORE trigger (`users_sessions_valid_after`); `endAllSessionsForUser` (so `endUserSessions` and T-039's reset) moves it to now. A trigger refuses moving it back or past `clock_timestamp()`, for any writer, so **re-enabling an account revives nothing**. An AFTER trigger (`users_end_sessions`) then deletes the dead rows; the cleanup deletes any left.
  - *Locks in one order: the user row, then session rows.* Making or copying a session holds the user row `FOR SHARE` (the insert trigger always; `switchOrganization` and `promoteSession` before they lock the session row). Every path that ends some or all of a user's sessions locks it first (`endOtherSessions` and `endAllSessionsForUser`: `FOR NO KEY UPDATE`; disabling: the UPDATE's own lock; removing a membership: the trigger locks it before its DELETE). So a copy being made either commits before the ending path reads, and is deleted, or starts after and finds its source gone or the account changed. `endOtherSessions` can't use the time (it keeps the caller's own session), so it relies on the lock: tested with the switch provably mid-transaction (a third connection holds the source row), which fails without the locks and passes with them. If the caller's *own* session is switched at the same moment, whichever commits first wins: either the other sessions end and the caller continues in the new organization, or the old token is already gone and `endOtherSessions` answers `no_session` (the admin asks again with the new token); the caller is never signed out by it.
  - Tests, on two pools, looped 30 times each: switch against sign-out-everywhere-else (by another session and by the caller's own), against an admin's end-all, against disable then re-enable; sign-in against disable then re-enable (both orders seen); 5 rounds of switching against removing the membership. Deleting a user or organization cascades; a membership removed ends the user's sessions in that organization. *Owner only:* `TRUNCATE organization_members` fires no row triggers, so it deletes no sessions; the lookups still refuse every session in an organization the user has left (they check the membership), but a waiting session with no organization chosen, or a session in an organization the user is later invited back to, would come back. Nothing in Oculus truncates that table, and the writer can't (no TRUNCATE grant). Password reset (T-039) must call `endAllSessionsForUser`; a password *change* offers `endOtherSessions` (D-052). No trigger fires on `password_hash`, because `signIn`'s re-hash writes it too.
- **A sign-in can't outrun a password change or "end all" (second review, R1).** `beginSession` checks the password (one statement reads the user row: the hash checked and `sessions_valid_after`) and only then makes the session, so a reset or "end all" that committed in between would have left a live session for the old password (29 rounds in 30 in the reviewer's race). Now `signInChecked` (accounts.ts, not exported from the index, so the hash never leaves the package) also reports the hash the password matched (the re-hashed one if it was re-hashed) and the exact `sessions_valid_after` it read with it; `beginSession` then, in one transaction, locks the user row `FOR SHARE` and requires the account to be active with **that same hash and that same `sessions_valid_after`**, and only then inserts. Anything else is `invalid_credentials`. That refusal is **not counted** against the throttle: the password was right when it was checked, and it is the account's change that won. `reauthenticate` stamps `password_at` only if the account still has the hash that was checked and the same `sessions_valid_after` (in the UPDATE's own conditions); otherwise `invalid_credentials` (or `no_session` if the session ended). Tests: the change pinned between the check and the insert (a reset, an "end all" alone and a password change alone, each on another connection), the same for re-entering the password, and a natural race of a sign-in against a reset, 30 rounds, after which no session from the old password is alive. The pinned tests and the race fail without the check.
- **A clock that steps back (second review, item 2).** Whenever `sessions_valid_after` moves, the trigger moves it past the latest `signed_in_at` of the user's sessions too, and the AFTER trigger deletes all of the user's sessions, whatever their stamps. `endAllSessionsForUser` moves the time before it deletes, so the trigger sees them. So a session stamped ahead of the clock (made before the server's clock stepped back) can't come back once the clock catches up. The price: until the clock passes that stamp, new sign-ins for that one account make no session (`invalid_credentials`); a clock stepping back by more than a few seconds is a fault to fix on the server anyway. Tested by moving a session an hour ahead as the owner, then disabling and re-enabling, and separately ending all.
- **Guards (0010), all `ENABLE ALWAYS` (D-067), tested in replica mode:** a new session is stamped now and needs an active user with access; a `second_step` one starts at its password; a `full` one can be inserted only as a copy of a live full session of the same user and sign-in (switching), so no writer can make one out of nothing. An update needs a live session and an active user with access; the stage never goes back; promotion changes the token and stamps the second step now; the organization, second step and end never change otherwise; `last_seen_at` and `password_at` only move forward and never past `clock_timestamp()`, and `password_at` changes only with a new token; a token change is stamped now; identity columns, the limits and the label never change (`zzzz_sessions_keep_identity`). So nobody revives an ended session or extends one. A trigger refusal raised in the instant between a query's own check and the trigger's (the clock moves inside a statement) is answered `NO_SESSION`.
- **Writer grants:** `sessions`: INSERT, UPDATE (also for `FOR UPDATE`), DELETE, in `WRITER_PRIVILEGES` through `SESSION_TABLE_PRIVILEGES`. Locking the user row uses the writer's existing UPDATE on `users`; no grant was added for it. A test runs a session's whole life as a role made by `grantWriterRole`, shows `checkWriterConnection` returns `[]` for it and `extra_privilege` for TRUNCATE, TRIGGER or REFERENCES on `sessions`, and that it can't disable triggers, truncate, enter replica mode, move a session or forge a full one. T-032's test that revokes each writer grant in turn now covers `sessions` too.
- **What the writer can still do with direct SQL, precisely** (beside D-067 and D-068). Until T-038 the database can't tell a real second step from a forged one, so the writer role can:
  - **make a full session for any active user**: insert a waiting session (stamped now, for a user and organization it may use; the insert trigger allows that), then UPDATE it to `full` with `second_step_at` now and a new hash of its choosing;
  - **copy a live full session** into a new row with a hash of its choosing (the insert trigger allows a copy of a live full session of the same sign-in, as switching makes), or simply set a live session's `token_hash` to one it knows;
  - **fake recent re-authentication**: set `password_at` to now, as long as it changes `token_hash` in the same UPDATE (the one cheap check added now; it stops a forged "recent" mark on a token someone else already holds, not one the writer makes for itself);
  - **keep a session from idling out** by moving `last_seen_at` up to now (never past the absolute end, which no update can move);
  - read every token hash (useless without the token), and delete any session or move a user's `sessions_valid_after` to now (sign anyone out).

  It can't: insert a full session that isn't a copy of a live one, revive an ended session, extend `expires_at`, move a session to another organization, keep a session of a disabled account, move `sessions_valid_after` back or past now, or skip any of this in replica mode. **T-038 must** bind the pending insert and promotion to real checks in the database: a row in its own table, written only when a code was checked, which promotion (and the switch copy) must find, so the writer can no longer promote by UPDATE. Until then the writer role's password is as sensitive as an admin's sign-in, as D-067 already says.
- **The cookie, for T-042 and T-045** (written only by `packages/api`'s `setOculusCookie`, D-058): `__Host-oculus_session=<token>; Path=/; HttpOnly; Secure; SameSite=Lax`, no `Domain`, and **no `Max-Age`** (a browser-session cookie; the server's limits decide when it ends, and a closed browser forgets it). `Lax` so a link from an email into the admin keeps the session; changes are guarded by the CSRF token and the Origin check, never by `SameSite` alone. `Strict` is allowed by the writer if T-045 prefers it. The admin is served from its own host (D-052, T-050). Every sign-in, promotion, rotation, switch and re-entry of the password writes the new token; sign-out clears it. The CSRF token is never put in a cookie. Responses already carry `Cache-Control: no-store` (D-058). Tokens follow D-068's secrecy rule: never in logs, URLs, error messages or responses other than the cookie.
- **Throttle notes from D-068, as built:** no remembered-device cookie or any throttle bypass (John, D-068); re-entering the password is throttled like sign-in. The warning when every request shows the same client address stays T-045's (it starts the sign-in route). The cleanup runs `deleteExpiredSignInThrottles` with `deleteExpiredSessions`; any scheduler runs `npm run db:cleanup` as the writer role (hourly is plenty, D-035).
- **Database failures never reach the caller as they are** (review item 5; second review item 3: the same rule as S-016). Every session function catches a thrown error that client.ts's `isDatabaseFailure` calls a database or connection failure (SQLSTATE classes 08, 40, 53, 57, 58, XX; a lost connection; a Node system error from the socket such as `ECONNREFUSED` or `ENOENT`; pg's own code-less connection errors) and answers a fixed `SessionFailure`: `retry` for class 40, `timeout` for 57014 and for a lock timeout (55P03, which T-051's `lock_timeout` raises, though class 55 isn't one of S-016's), `database_unavailable` for the rest. 0010's own session guards answer `NO_SESSION`. The raw error goes only to the `onError` option (a throw from it is ignored); no token or hash is ever put into an error or a result. Everything else (a permission or syntax error, `ERR_*`, a bug, a bad option) is thrown, as elsewhere in the package. `internal_error` is only for a statement that returned no row. `deleteExpiredSessions` and `runCleanup` throw, and the command prints only the SQLSTATE.
- **Timing:** an unknown token, an expired one, a signed-out one and a waiting one used as full each cost one indexed statement and give the same frozen `NO_SESSION` (tested: medians within a factor of 2.5, plus half a millisecond). Only someone who already holds a token can make a known one, so this guards little, but nothing branches in code on which case it was.
- **Left for later:** HTTP routes (T-042/T-045), the CSRF check on routes (T-042), T-038's second step, role changes calling `rotateSession` (T-041), site unlocks on the session row (T-019d), the policy from System settings (T-048), a sign-in and session event log, and a cap on parallel sessions (D-052 sets none).
- **Rejected:** keeping the pending state in a separate table (two lookups and two cleanups for one sign-in); one token for both stages (a token seen at the code screen would become a full session's); taking a `SignInSuccess` object to make a session (any code can build one; `beginSession` runs the check itself); extending the absolute end on promotion or switching (switching back and forth would keep a session alive forever); refreshing `last_seen_at` only once a minute (fewer writes, but idle precision is part of the limit; revisit if measured).
- Sources: OWASP ASVS 5.0 V7 (7.1.1, 7.1.2, 7.2.1–7.2.4, 7.3.1, 7.3.2, 7.4.1–7.4.5, 7.5.1–7.5.3) and V3.3 (3.3.1–3.3.4); OWASP Session Management and Cross-Site Request Forgery Prevention cheat sheets (HMAC-based tokens, re-authentication); RFC 4648 §5 (base64url) and §3.5 (canonical encoding, non-zero pad bits); RFC 6265bis §4.1.3 (`__Host-`); PostgreSQL 16 docs: `sql-createtrigger.html`, `sql-altertable.html` (`ENABLE ALWAYS TRIGGER`), `functions-datetime.html` (`clock_timestamp()` vs `now()`); Node.js `crypto` docs (`createHmac`, `timingSafeEqual`, `randomBytes`). Read 2026-11-23 from the versions already cited in D-052, D-058 and D-068.

## D-071 — Stored terms carry their organization in core; Data Cleaning's term lists are lists of one organization's entries, checked against the organization the caller expects (2026-11-23, autopilot T-019b2)
Closes the last part of D-030's "vocabularies and terms … follow the same rule" (T-019b2). In the database, terms were already one organization's (D-062: `organization_id` in every key and foreign key, identity triggers); what was missing was the core side. Migrations: none (no schema change, so D-067's writer allowlist and ENABLE ALWAYS rule are untouched).
- **`StoredTerm` in core** (`stored-terms.ts`): `{ organizationId, id, term }`, the shape of a `terms` row, checked by `validateStoredTerm` (a plain object, its prototype `Object.prototype` or null and not a Proxy, with exactly those three own enumerable data properties, so no getters, setters, hidden properties, arrays, Dates or class instances, `invalid_organization`, `invalid_id`, the term by `validateControlledTerm`). `validateStoredTerms(list, organizationId)` checks an organization's whole stored list: any entry not of that organization refuses it with `mixed_organizations` before anything else is read (the S-009b order), then duplicate IDs (`duplicate_id`) and two entries that are one term (`duplicate_term`). `packages/db`'s `StoredTerm` is now core's plus `termKey`, and its `TermUsage` and `RecordTerms` carry `organizationId` too.
- **One rule for "the same term".** `termIdentity(term)` is the text D-062 described (vocabulary plus `termUriKey`, or vocabulary plus exact label); `packages/db` hashes it for `term_key` instead of building its own copy, so `duplicate_term` and `terms_one_per_key` can't drift apart. The key's bytes are unchanged, so stored keys stay valid. A test pins them: fixed SHA-256 hex values (and the exact `termIdentity` strings) for a label term, a local term and two URI terms, computed with `packages/db`'s old `keyOf` from before this change, so any later change to the rule fails a test and must come with a migration. (A separate test only checks that `saveTerm` stores the hash of `termIdentity`, which says nothing about the bytes being the old ones.)
- **Data Cleaning's set functions take owned entries and the expected organization.** `findNearDuplicateTerms(input, organizationId, options)` and `findLcTermsWithoutUri(input, organizationId, options)` now take `OrganizationTerm` entries (`{ organizationId, term }`; a stored term or a usage from the index will do, other properties are ignored) and refuse, with one fixed-text `mixed_organizations` issue and before any term is read, any entry not of `organizationId`: another organization's, one with no, an invalid, a getter or a hidden `organizationId`, anything but a plain object (a Proxy, an array, a Date, a class instance), a bare term, a hole, a getter or hidden slot. A term behind a getter or hidden is skipped as `not_plain_data`, as a getter or hidden list item was before. Why it matters here more than elsewhere: a duplicate pair's message names both labels, so a pooled list would show one organization's labels to the other's staff, and the report itself would invite merging across organizations. A bad term in an entry of the right organization is still that entry's problem only (`skipped`, paths now `[i, "term", …]`).
  - *Why the expected organization as an argument* (D-030 rejected a per-call `organizationId` in general): as S-009b found for identifier types, entries alone can't tell a list wholly another organization's from the caller's own, and these reports quote what they read. The argument is what the caller expects (from the session, via the service), checked before the data is read; it is not a second copy of the data's own organization.
  - *Rejected:* keeping bare `ControlledTerm` lists (nothing could refuse a pooled list); one `{ organizationId, terms }` container (D-030's reason: the data itself couldn't show it was loaded from the wrong organization); filtering out strays (D-030: a filtered report looks complete). *Breaking:* both functions' second argument is now the organization; nothing outside tests called them.
- **Rights settings: none are stored yet.** The rights statements are the built-in `RIGHTS_TERMS`, and a record's rights value is part of the record (which carries its organization). When an organization's own rights settings are added (which statements it offers, a default, wording), they carry a required `organizationId` and their set functions refuse mixed or unowned entries in the same way, and their table follows D-060's keys.
- Sources: none beyond the earlier decisions (D-014, D-030, D-062, S-009b); no standard governs this.

## D-072 — Roles and collection-level permissions: built-in roles as fixed permission sets, granted per site (reaching that site's items, D-074) and per unit's subtree, checked in each write's transaction under the person's row lock; sites and System settings for system managers (2026-11-24, autopilot T-041)
Builds D-019, D-025, D-030 and D-036 on D-067's service layer and D-068's memberships. Pure rules in `packages/core/src/permissions.ts` and `system-settings.ts`; data access in `packages/db/src/permissions.ts`; tables in `packages/db/migrations/0013_roles.sql` (numbered after 0010, T-037's sessions, and 0012, T-034b/c's workflow states, which merged first: the runner refuses a migration sorting before one already applied; 0011 is unused); the checks in `packages/service/src/access.ts`, `service.ts` and `admin.ts`. No new dependency.
- **Permissions are a fixed list; roles are fixed sets of them; the service checks permissions, never role names** (D-036). Catalog permissions (granted for the whole catalog or a unit's subtree): `records.view`, `records.create`, `records.edit`, `records.delete`, `records.history`, `records.publish`, `media.attach`, `donors.view`, `donors.edit`. Organization permissions (whole-catalog grants only): none since John's decision below (`catalog.definitions` was one). Site permissions (that site, or every site): `site.settings`, `site.people`. System permissions (`system.settings`, `system.sites`, `system.users`, `system.plugins`, and since 2026-11-24 `catalog.definitions`) belong to system managers (`users.system_admin`) only; no role can hold one, built-in or (later, T-041a) custom.
- **The built-in roles** (written out in a test, so any change shows):

  | Role | view | create | edit | delete | history | publish | attach scans | donors | site settings, site people |
  |---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
  | site manager | ✓ | ✓ | ✓ | ✓ | ✓ | ✓ | ✓ | view, edit | ✓ |
  | cataloger | ✓ | ✓ | ✓ | | ✓ | | ✓ | | |
  | reviewer | ✓ | ✓ | ✓ | | ✓ | ✓ | ✓ | | |
  | read-only | ✓ | | | | | | | | |
  | Scanner | ✓ | | | | | | ✓ | | |
  | Donor relations | | | | | | | | view, edit | |

  From D-025, D-036 and the mockup's Settings ("Catalogers can add and edit records; only Reviewers and Administrators can publish"). Two of these were the autopilot's choices and **John confirmed both (2026-11-24)**: deleting catalog records is site managers' (and system managers') only; read-only users do not see old versions. A third stays the autopilot's: Donor relations sees no catalog records yet ("the records an accession produced", D-036, needs stored accessions, T-018b). `media.attach` lets Scanner make and change digital assets only (a scan's own record), never other kinds; anyone editing an asset needs `records.edit` or `media.attach`. **One rule for numbers** (review): whoever may change a record may set its identifiers, by `saveIdentifiers` or with a save (`createRecord`, `updateRecord`) alike, so Scanners set numbers on digital assets only, by every route (tested). **John (2026-11-24): templates, record types, identifier types and vocabularies (`catalog.definitions`) can be changed by system managers only.** It answers the question that was open here (whether being site manager of one site gives control of the whole organization's definitions): no. `catalog.definitions` moved from the organization permissions to the system permissions in core (`permissions.ts`), so no role, built-in or custom (T-041a), can hold it; the site manager row above lost it, and the role test lists the change.
- **Where a role is held.** `role_assignments (organization_id, id, user_id, role, site_id, scope_record_id, granted_by, granted_at)`: one built-in role for one member of one organization (composite foreign key to `organization_members`, so no role without a membership and none across organizations), on one site (`site_id`, composite foreign key to that organization's `sites`) or on every site of it (`NULL`), for the whole catalog (`scope_record_id NULL`) or for one archival unit and everything below it (composite foreign key to that organization's `records`; a trigger requires `kind = 'archival_unit'`, which never changes). A site manager is always whole-catalog (CHECK). One row per role, site and scope (`UNIQUE NULLS NOT DISTINCT`, PostgreSQL 15+). Rows never change (identity trigger on every column; the writer has no UPDATE): revoking deletes, changing is a delete and an insert. Catalog permissions reach the records whose home site is the grant's site (every site's, for a grant on every site; D-074, below); organization permissions come from a whole-catalog grant on any site; site permissions count only on their site. *Rejected:* site-only assignments (D-019's "set a role per site" is covered, and a role on every site saves a row per new site); a soft-revoke flag (D-067: a forgotten filter would keep revoked access).
- **Items belong to a site (D-074, John, 2026-11-24).** Every record has a home site: `records.site_id`, nullable, with a composite foreign key `records_home_site` to its own organization's `sites` (0013; never another organization's, even for a writer with its guard triggers off, tested), carried in every version through `record_snapshot` as `siteId` (left out when NULL, so versions written before still equal their rows), in core's `CatalogRecord.siteId`, and compared and listed in `changed_fields` like any property. **A catalog permission granted on a site reaches only the records whose home site is that site; one granted on every site reaches every site's records; records on no site are reached by no role, only by system managers** (who can give them a site). A unit scope narrows within the grant's site. So the table above reads per site: a site manager of the Library site manages the Library's items, not the Society's. Definitions (`catalog.definitions`) are system managers' only (John, 2026-11-24, above). **The working site:** `ServiceContext.siteId` (optional; a session doesn't carry it yet, so the caller passes it until T-045's selector does) is where `createRecord` puts a new record. It must be one of the organization's sites on which the person holds a role (or every site), else `unknown_site`, the same answer as for a site that doesn't exist or another organization's; without one only a system manager makes records (on no site); a draft may name only the working site (`wrong_site`). Other operations are checked at the record's own home site with all the person's grants, so someone on two sites sees and edits both sites' items from either. An update draft that leaves out `siteId` keeps the home site; naming another is a **move between sites**, checked like a move in the arrangement: edit rights where it leaves and where it goes (`unknown_site` for a site they have no role on), so only system managers or someone with edit rights on both sites move items; restoring a version on another site is the same move. Parents and represented records are locked `FOR SHARE` and must be viewable by the person (on their own site), or the answer is the one for a missing parent or represented record, so nothing reaches across sites by filing, scanning or linking (tested, each equal to the answer for a random ID). **Room for sharing (T-041b):** every permission target carries `sharedInto`, the sites that accepted the record; a grant there gives `records.view` only, never a change; `sitesSharedInto` (packages/db) returns no sites until T-041b stores accepted offers. Tests: visibility per site for every role, a person on two sites, every-site grants, no-site items, creation into the working site, moves between sites with their history, cross-site parents, scans and links.
- **Second review (D-074 amendments).**
  - *A grant's unit is on the grant's site.* A scope must be a unit whose home site is the grant's site (any site of the organization for a grant on every site; never a unit on no site), for everyone, system managers included, and for anyone else a unit they may view. Any other scope (another site's, one on no site, one out of sight, one that doesn't exist) gets the same `unknown_record`, checked before the kind, so `not_a_unit` says nothing about records the actor can't see. The unit's row is locked `FOR SHARE` (after the arrangement lock) so its site can't change before COMMIT. Before this, a site manager could scope a grant to another site's unit, which then blocked that site's manager from deleting it with grants they couldn't list.
  - *A unit with grants on its site doesn't change site.* Moving a unit to another site while roles on its current site are scoped to it is refused for everyone (`has_role_grants`, no count) until those grants are revoked; grants on every site still hold after the move and stay. *Rejected:* deleting the grants in the same transaction (a silent loss of access, and no audit trail yet, T-048).
  - *Items stay on one site.* For anyone but a system manager, a unit is filed only under a unit of its own home site and a scan represents only a record of its own site (`other_site` when they can see the parent or record; one they can't see is still missing), whether made, moved or restored, so inherited values and a scan's place never cross sites. A record with units filed under it or scans of it doesn't change site except by a system manager (`in_use_below`). System managers may file across sites. Moving a whole subtree between sites in one step is left for later; today a system manager does it.
  - *A change of home site takes the arrangement lock* (exclusive, before the row lock) like a parent or `represents` change, so a scoped check of links (`hiddenAmong`) never sees it half done (tested with the move held mid-way).
  - *Restoring a version written before the record had a site keeps its current home site* (an absent `siteId` in an update draft or an old version means unchanged); restoring a version on another real site is a move needing edit rights on both.
  - *Deletion blockers for anyone but a system manager* (units filed under it, scans of it, links to it, grants on it) become one fixed `in_use` with no count or kind, since what blocks may be on a site the actor can't see; `holds_identifiers` and `published` are about the record itself and stay. System managers still get the counts.
  - *Listing grants:* anyone but a system manager, a site manager on every site included, lists only what they may revoke (no site manager grants, no grants on every site).
- **What a scoped grant covers.** A record is in a unit's subtree when it is that unit or below it by `parentId`, or is a digital asset representing such a record (following assets of assets up to 8 hops). Objects, agents, places and events are outside the arrangement, so only whole-catalog grants cover them, and only whole-catalog grants make new fonds. **Moving** (a unit's parent, an asset's represented record, including by restoring an old version) needs the edit permission at the place it leaves and the place it goes, never counting the record's own grant: someone granted only a series can edit it but not move it out. **Links in fields** (`record_link`, places): a link the save *adds* to a record the actor may not view is checked as a link to a record that doesn't exist (`unknown_record`, the same answer word for word; review: `wrong_record_kind` against `unknown_record` told a scoped person that a record exists and its kind). `RecordAccess.hiddenAmong` finds them (only for people with scoped grants, under the arrangement lock) and `recordCheckIssues`' `hiddenLinks` leaves them out of the link checker; restores pass the same. Links the record already holds and keeps are checked as before, so a scoped cataloger can still edit an item whose creator they can't open. A scoped cataloger who must link authority records (agents and places are outside the arrangement) needs a whole-catalog read-only grant beside the scoped one. **Uniqueness still answers** (review, accepted as inherent): "already used" for an identifier can show that a number exists on a record the actor can't see; a uniqueness rule can't be kept without saying so, and the answer names no record. Screens should not offer it as a search. *Rejected:* a permission per record (no rule an archive would state; grants follow the arrangement as archivists describe it); counting the unit's own grant for moves (a grant on one series would let its holder file it under any fonds).
- **Checked in the write's transaction, before the record is touched.** Each operation (`createRecord`, `updateRecord`, `saveIdentifiers`, `restoreVersion`, `deleteRecord`, and the new `readRecord`) first reads the actor's access with `loadAccess`: **the person's user row locked `FOR SHARE`, then (a new statement, a new snapshot under Read Committed) their system manager flag, disabled state and grants.** Every role change locks the person's row `FOR NO KEY UPDATE` (`lockPeopleForRoleChange`), and disabling an account is an UPDATE of that row. A grant or revocation first checks the actor and the person without locks (the person's membership before any lock, so no outsider's row is ever locked), then locks the actor's and the person's rows in ID order (one row when they are the same person, so there is no FOR SHARE to FOR NO KEY UPDATE upgrade) and checks again under the locks (review: A granting to B while B grants to A, or one person given two roles at once, deadlocked; tested with `attempts: 1`, no `retry`). So a revocation committed before is seen; one in flight makes the write wait and then see it; a write already checked finishes before the revocation commits. Writes by one person share the lock and don't wait for each other. Someone with any scoped grant also takes the organization's arrangement lock (D-065) before reading any record's place, so no unit moves until COMMIT (scoped writes in one organization therefore run one at a time; whole-catalog and system-manager writes take no extra lock). **Reads take it shared** (review): `readRecord` takes `arrangement_lock_shared` (0013, the same advisory key in shared mode), so scoped reads run side by side; writes keep it exclusive (they may move what they write, and a shared lock can't safely be raised). **Every move takes it exclusively, before the row lock**: a unit's parent or level (as before) and now also a digital asset's `represents` (review: an asset could move between a scoped person's check and write), in `updateRecord` and in `restoreVersion` alike. **The record's row is locked before its place is read** (review): `readRecord` (FOR SHARE), `saveIdentifiers`, `updateRecord`, `restoreVersion` (FOR NO KEY UPDATE) and `deleteRecord` (FOR UPDATE) lock the row, then read where it is and check there; a restore's move check runs from that place. `updateRecord` and `restoreVersion` first look at the row unlocked only to decide whether to take the arrangement lock, and start again (`retry`) if the version under the lock differs from that look while still matching the expected one. Tests pin each race with a lock held on a third connection: an owner moving a scan out of a collection while a scoped cataloger's `saveIdentifiers` or `restoreVersion` waits (refused as unknown, nothing written), a scan's move waiting for a scoped write already checked, two scoped reads at once, and a read waiting for a move in flight. Tests show each lock is needed: without the `FOR SHARE`, a write starting during a revocation, a write already checked and a disable all go wrong; without the arrangement lock, a scoped write racing a move out of the collection goes through. *Rejected:* locking the assignment rows (`FOR SHARE` there needs UPDATE on the table and misses new grants and disables); trusting a session's role list or `systemAdmin` flag (it can be stale; the database is read every time, and `contextFromSession` passes only the person and organization).
- **Refusals reveal nothing the actor can't see.** A record the actor may not view is answered exactly as one that doesn't exist (`unknown_record`, tested as equal results for every operation); a parent or represented record they may not view, exactly as a missing one (`missing_parent`, `unknown_record`); a role assignment they can't manage, exactly as an unknown ID; a person who isn't a member (or doesn't exist), the same `unknown_person`. `not_permitted` ("You don't have permission to do this.") only where they can see what they asked about. No message names a record, person or site. A deletion's counts (D-067) are seen only by people with `records.delete`, today whole-catalog site managers. The database-role refusal (SQLSTATE 42501) that D-067 called `not_permitted` is now `writer_role_refused`, so the code means one thing.
- **Workflow actions (T-034b/c, D-076) under these permissions.** `publish` and `withdraw`, and confirming values public on a published record, ask `ServiceOptions.mayChangePublicOutput`, whose default is now `publishPermission` (access.ts; T-034 shipped a default allowing everyone until this merge): `records.publish` at the record's place (home site, units). Asked before the operation has looked the record up, it looks it up itself in the session's organization: a record that doesn't exist, one of another organization and one the actor can't view are all `unknown_record`, word for word; one they can see, `not_permitted`. It changes no data; it takes the locks the operation takes next anyway, in the same order (the arrangement lock for scoped grants, then the record's row `FOR NO KEY UPDATE`, as `changeWorkflowState` does), so the place it checked holds until COMMIT. `submitForReview` and `returnToDraft` need the edit permission where the record is (`records.edit`, or `media.attach` for a scan), checked under the row lock like any edit. Tests: each role, a person on another site, a scoped reviewer, and the hook in a transaction of its own leaving the record and its versions as they were.
- **Who grants.** System managers grant and revoke any built-in role in any organization, on one site or every site, scoped or not. Site managers (`site.people`) grant, revoke and list roles below site manager on their own sites, one site at a time (`listRoleAssignments` shows them only those: not grants on every site, which they can't revoke; it pages, `after` and `limit` up to 1000, instead of stopping silently at 10 000) (D-025: they manage their site's people; system managers decide which sites each person can use and appoint site managers). Granting checks the actor before reading anything about the person, site or unit. Jobs (`system:` actors, D-063) hold no roles and are refused everything until a task (an importer) gives them a defined scope.
- **Sites** (`sites (organization_id, id, name, created_at)`, D-030's `Site`): keyed `(organization_id, id)` with no key on `id` alone (D-060), name checked as `organizations.name`, identity trigger on organization, ID and creation, nothing deletes them. Created and renamed only by system managers (`createSite`, `renameSite`), in any organization (System settings are install-wide, D-019). T-019d adds site passwords here.
- **System settings** (D-019): one row (`system_settings`, `singleton`), a JSON document core's `validateSystemSettings` accepts (every key present, no unknown key, each in bounds), a version that every change raises by exactly 1 (trigger), who changed it and when (stamped now). Today it holds the session limits D-052 and D-069 make configurable: `sessionIdleMinutes` (5–720, default 240) and `sessionAbsoluteMinutes` (60–1440, default 720), idle ≤ absolute; before the first change the defaults read as version 0. Email, backups, updates and site passwords add their own keys in their tasks. `readSystemSettings`/`changeSystemSettings` (with `expectedVersion`) are system managers' only; everyone else is refused before anything is read. Since T-037's merge, `beginSession` takes its limits from here when the caller passes none (an unreadable document, an out-of-bounds value or a failed read gives the defaults; a sign-in is never refused for it), tested.
- **After sign-in** (`listMySites`, `sitesForPerson`): an active system manager sees every site; anyone else the sites of their organizations they hold a role on (a role on every site counts for each), across organizations, ordered by organization and site name; a disabled or unknown account, none. The site selector (T-045) uses it.
- **Writer grants, in `WRITER_PRIVILEGES`** through `PERMISSION_TABLE_PRIVILEGES`: `sites` INSERT, UPDATE; `role_assignments` INSERT, DELETE; `system_settings` INSERT, UPDATE. Locking a person's row uses the writer's existing UPDATE on `users`. Tests: `checkWriterConnection` accepts exactly these and reports `extra_privilege` for UPDATE or TRUNCATE on `role_assignments` and DELETE on `sites` or `system_settings`; the writer gets 42501 for each. Every trigger in 0013 is `ENABLE ALWAYS` (tested, and in replica mode as the superuser).
- **What the writer can still do with direct SQL** (beside D-067 to D-069): move any record to another site of the same organization, or to none, with a save version (the foreign key only keeps it inside the organization; the same-site and grant rules above are the service's); grant any built-in role to any member of an organization, on any of its sites and units, in anyone's name (`granted_by` is any user); revoke any role; rename sites, make new ones; change System settings (only to a document whose version is the next one, stamped now; the JSON's content is checked only when read, which refuses an invalid one as `unreadable_settings`). It can't: give a role to a non-member or across organizations, scope one to anything but a unit of the same organization, make a site manager of a collection, change an assignment in place, move a site to another organization, delete a site or the settings, or skip any of this in replica mode. As D-067 says, the writer role's password is as sensitive as a system manager's sign-in.
- **Left for later:** making and removing system managers (users.system_admin is still the bootstrap account only, D-068; T-048's Users page needs a rule for who may and at least one active manager left, and a migration replacing that CHECK); a log of role changes (T-048's audit trail: today a grant keeps who and when, a revocation leaves no trace); rotating the affected person's sessions on a role change (D-069 asks T-041 to call `rotateSession`: permissions are read live in every operation, so nothing stale is used, but T-037's merge should end or rotate the person's sessions on revocation if wanted); invitations by site managers (`createInvitation` still needs a system manager); **recent re-authentication for admin operations** (review): granting and revoking roles, creating and renaming sites and changing System settings should require the person to have entered their password recently, as T-037's `endUserSessions` does; T-048 (and T-048a) add it when the screens call these; permission checks for templates, record types, identifier types and vocabularies (`catalog.definitions`, T-047), publishing (`records.publish`, T-034), donors and accessions (T-018b), and reads beyond `readRecord` (search, history, compare: T-042/T-043 call the same checks); T-041a custom roles.
- Sources: PostgreSQL 16 docs: `explicit-locking.html` (row-level lock conflicts: `FOR SHARE` against `FOR NO KEY UPDATE`), `sql-select.html` (locking clause; under Read Committed a waiting `FOR SHARE` returns the updated row), `transaction-iso.html` (each statement's own snapshot), `sql-createtable.html` (`UNIQUE NULLS NOT DISTINCT`, PostgreSQL 15), `sql-createtrigger.html` and `sql-altertable.html` (`ENABLE ALWAYS TRIGGER`). The PostgreSQL pages are the versions D-063, D-065 and D-067 already cite. OWASP ASVS 5.0's authorization chapter (V8) could not be fetched in this run (github.com was not reachable); the rules followed here (deny by default, check on every request at the trusted service layer, answer what may not be seen as if it didn't exist) are the ones D-067 and D-068 already apply, and the security review should check them against V8.
- **Amendment (2026-11-24, manual session, S-022): the database backs up who may grant.** `role_assignments_check_new` (0013) checked only the scope's kind and the time, so any writer could record a grant "by" anyone. Migration `0018_role_grant_guard.sql` adds `role_assignments_check_grant` (BEFORE INSERT, ENABLE ALWAYS, `search_path` pinned), mirroring `invitations_check_new` for `invited_by` and the service's `mayManage`: `granted_by` must be an active user (its row locked `FOR SHARE`, so a change to their own roles that locks it waits) who is a system manager, or, for a role below `site_manager` on one site, a member holding a whole-catalog `site_manager` grant (the only role with `site.people`; a test compares the list with core's `ROLE_PERMISSIONS`) on that site or on every site of the organization (`role_assignments_granted_by`). `site_manager` grants and every-site grants need a system manager. A scope unit must be on the grant's site, or on some site for an every-site grant (`role_assignments_scope_site`), as the service already required. Tested as the writer role (cataloger, reviewer, no-role and other-organization granters; a site manager granting on another site, on every site or a site manager; a disabled site manager; another site's unit even for a system manager) and in replica mode. Like the invitations check, it reads committed state: a direct writer that deletes the granter's own role without locking their user row can still race it, which the service never does.

## D-073 — The React admin: Vite 7 with React 19, React Router, TanStack Query, React Aria Components styled with the design tokens, an in-house template form renderer checked by the server, self-hosted fonts (2026-11-24, autopilot T-044)

- **Context.** T-044 asks how the staff admin is built and served in development. D-034 answered where it lives in production: one app, with Fastify serving the built admin on the admin's own hostname. D-052 and D-058 set the session cookie (`__Host-oculus_session`, SameSite=Lax, a CSRF token per session) and the origin check.
- **Build.** React 19 and react-dom, built with the pinned Vite 7 (D-006) through `@vitejs/plugin-react-swc` 4 (works with Vite 7; the main React plugin's current line needs Vite 8). The admin is its own workspace package with the shared strict TypeScript config plus `jsx: react-jsx`, the DOM libraries and `moduleResolution: bundler`; `eslint-plugin-react-hooks` joins the lint rules.
- **Development.** The Vite dev server forwards `/api` to Fastify on 127.0.0.1 with `changeOrigin: false`, so the browser sees one origin (`http://localhost:5173`, listed in `OCULUS_ORIGINS`, which D-058 allows on localhost) and the Origin, Host, cookie and CSRF checks run unchanged. Safari may need local HTTPS for the `Secure` cookie. Vite's hot-reload WebSocket exists only in development. In production the admin gets its own CSP (`script-src 'self'; style-src 'self'`), separate from the API's `default-src 'none'`; T-050 sets it.
- **Routing:** React Router 8 as a plain library (`createBrowserRouter`), not its framework mode. *Rejected:* TanStack Router (typed routes, but more parts and a code-generation step).
- **Data:** TanStack Query 5, behind one small fetch wrapper that adds the CSRF header and `If-Match` for edit conflicts and turns `{ error }` replies into errors; 4xx replies are not retried. *Rejected:* plain fetch (caching and refresh after saves rewritten per screen).
- **Forms from templates:** a small in-house renderer, one component per field kind in `FIELD_KINDS`, form state in `useReducer`, each core `Issue.path` mapped to its field so problems show inline. **John (2026-11-24): checks come from the server.** A dry-run "check" endpoint returns core's `Issue[]` when a field loses focus and before saving, so core stays the only judge and no validator is copied. (`@oculus/core` imports `node:util`, `node:crypto` and `node:net`, so it can't be bundled for the browser as it is; a browser build could come later if round trips feel slow.) *Rejected:* react-hook-form and TanStack Form (their own validation model beside core's).
- **Components and styling:** React Aria Components (Apache-2.0, Adobe) for accessible widgets (ComboBox for controlled terms and record links, Tabs, Dialog, Select), styled with plain CSS and CSS Modules using `design/tokens/tokens.css`, with no CSS-in-JS (runtime `<style>` tags would need a looser `style-src`). React Aria's coming 3.0 will need its own entry. *Rejected:* Radix (no ComboBox), Base UI (younger), our own widgets (accessibility risk).
- **Testing:** component tests in Vitest with jsdom and Testing Library (they count toward `tests_passing`; `vitest.config.js` adds `*.test.tsx`); end-to-end tests with Playwright against the built admin served by Fastify, as a new gate step after the build. **John (2026-11-24): accessibility checks use Siteimprove Alfa** (`@siteimprove/alfa-playwright`, `@siteimprove/alfa-rules` and their peers, MIT and Apache-2.0), not axe-core, whose MPL-2.0 license D-006 doesn't allow even for test tools. Keyboard and screen-reader checks stay manual (T-045).
- **Fonts. John (2026-11-24): host them ourselves.** Roboto and Open Sans are under the SIL Open Font License 1.1, which is allowed **for font files only**: they are served from the admin's own origin (no request to Google, no CSP exception), with the OFL notice kept beside them. OFL stays disallowed for code and npm dependencies. T-045 adds the files and extends the license check to font files.
- **Versions at the time of the decision (2026-11-24):** `react` and `react-dom` 19.3.1, `react-router` 8.5.2, `@tanstack/react-query` 5.108.4, `react-aria-components` 1.22.1; dev: `@vitejs/plugin-react-swc` 4.3.3, `@types/react` and `@types/react-dom` 19.3.0, `eslint-plugin-react-hooks` 7.1.1, `@testing-library/react` 16.3.3, `@testing-library/dom` 10.4.2, `@testing-library/user-event` 14.6.13, `jsdom` 31.0.1, `@playwright/test` 1.65.1, `@siteimprove/alfa-playwright` 0.84.4, `@siteimprove/alfa-rules` and peers 0.120.0. A trial install found every package MIT, Apache-2.0 or 0BSD and `npm audit` clean; React, the router and Query came to about 111 KB gzipped, ten React Aria components about 71 KB more.
- Sources: npm registry metadata on 2026-11-24; React Router v8 announcement (remix.run/blog/react-router-v8).

## D-075 — Workflow states: four record-level states, four actions, a published record leaves the public only by withdrawing; per-site showing comes later beside the state (2026-11-24, autopilot T-034a)
T-034 was split (T-034a to T-034e); this is the first part, pure and in core (`workflow.ts`). The stored state, the service's actions and the publish checks are T-034b's.
