- **States** (the mockup's Items list and record editor, DESIGN.md's pills): `draft` (Draft), `ready_for_review` (Ready for review), `published` (Published), `withdrawn` (Withdrawn). A new record starts as `draft`. Only `published` is public (`isPublicState`); anything else, including a value that isn't a state, is not public. Names are matched exactly (no trimming or case folding); messages are fixed text and never quote input.
- **Actions and the allowed moves** (`workflowTransition(from, to)`, `applyWorkflowAction(from, action)`, `workflowMoves(from)`): `submit_for_review` (draft or withdrawn → ready_for_review), `return_to_draft` (ready_for_review or withdrawn → draft), `publish` (draft, ready_for_review or withdrawn → published), `withdraw` (published → withdrawn, labelled "Unpublish" as in DESIGN.md's Publish ↔ Unpublish). Nothing else; a move to the same state is `no_change`.
- **A published record leaves the public only by `withdraw`**, never straight to draft or review, so taking a record off the public sites is always one recognisable action that needs the publish permission. `changesPublicOutput(action)` marks `publish` and `withdraw`; T-041 checks the publish permission (D-036) for those two and only the edit permission for the others.
- **Publishing from `draft` is allowed**, because the approval step is optional and off by default (T-035, a future feature). When that step is on, it narrows `publish` to `ready_for_review` records; the table stays the same.
- **Saving, restoring a version (D-065) and changing identifiers never change the state.** Only the four actions do. For T-034b: an edit or restore of a published record re-runs the publish check (D-065's note) and is refused if it would fail it, rather than quietly withdrawing; a published record must be withdrawn before it is deleted; each state change is written in the change log with its actor.
- **Per-site publishing later (Phase 5, "Show on these sites")** goes beside the state, not into it: the state stays one value per record, and a later set of (record, site) rows says on which of the organization's sites a published record shows. A record shows on a site only while its state is `published`. So adding sites needs a new table, not a rewrite of the state.
- **Rejected:** a boolean `published` flag (no room for review or withdrawn, and the mockup has both); letting a published record move straight back to draft (unpublishing would then be an edit-permission action, around D-036's publish permission); a state per site now (no sites table until T-041, and Phase 5 decides how sites choose records); an `archived` or `deleted` state (deletion is a tombstone version, D-067).
- Sources: the mockup (Items, AdminRecord) and DESIGN.md §2 and §7; no standard governs workflow states.

## D-074 — Items belong to a site: people see and edit only the items of the sites they're assigned to, system managers see everything, and an item can be offered to another site (even another organization's) for that site to accept, staying owned by its home site (2026-11-24, John)

- **Context.** In Omeka S every item is in one pool, several sites pull from it, and a user with access sees the whole pool. John wants Oculus to work differently (asked while T-041 was in review).
- **John (2026-11-24):**
  - *Items have a home site.* Every catalog record (item) belongs to one primary site. A person assigned to a site (site manager or any other role) sees and works only with the items of that site; other sites and their items are invisible to them. Someone assigned to two sites (for example the Library site and the DHS site) can go to each and see and edit its items.
  - *System managers see everything*, including items that are on no site yet. Items on no site are visible only to system managers, who can assign them to a site.
  - *New items join the site they're made in.* An item Joe creates while working in the Library site is the Library site's.
  - *Sharing.* A site's people can offer an item to another site. That site's manager reviews the offer and accepts or declines it. An accepted item shows on the second site too, but the **home site takes precedence**: the item keeps the home site's data, and the second site displays it as it is, saying which site (and organization) it belongs to. John chose that **sharing works across organizations** too (for example Library to DHS), while DHS and the Library otherwise stay separate as D-019 says: their own ID numbers, rights, people and roles.
  - *Editing a shared item.* John: a person who manages the item's site sees and edits it, and the change shows on every site displaying it. Read with "the home site takes precedence", edits come from the home site's people; the people of a site that accepted the item see and display it (and can remove it from their own site) but don't change its data.
- **What this changes.** D-072's roles stay, but their reach becomes per site: a role on a site covers the items whose home site it is (and, read-only, items accepted into it), rather than the organization's whole catalog. An "every site" grant covers the whole organization's items. D-019's "nothing is shared" gains one deliberate exception: an accepted offer lets a second site, possibly in another organization, display an item read-only with its owner named. The item itself, its ID numbers and its history stay with its home organization.
- **Tasks.** T-041 makes the home site part of every record and scopes roles by site. A new task, T-041b, builds offering, reviewing, accepting, declining and withdrawing items between sites, including across organizations, and how a shared item reads and displays on the second site.
- **Accepted one-bit signals (T-041 third review, 2026-11-24).** To someone who already has edit rights on a record, a few refusals say only that something they can't see is involved, with no count, kind or ID: `in_use` on deletion, `in_use_below` when moving a unit with hidden records below it to another site, `has_role_grants` when moving a unit that has roles scoped to it, and `unknown_site` when restoring a version filed on a site they have no role on. Lock waits on rows held for invisible records are an inherent timing signal. These are accepted.
- **Amendment (2026-11-24, manual session, S-021): identifier numbers are the organization's.** Two more accepted signals. (1) **Uniqueness:** a number (current, or former under `all_time`) held by a record on a site the person can't see is refused as `not_unique` ("… is already used by another record"), because uniqueness is per organization (D-061: one unique index per rule over the organization's holdings) and a number can't be given to two records just because their sites differ; hiding the clash would mean saving a duplicate. The reply is **byte-for-byte the reply for a clash with a record they can see**, except for the number they typed: no record ID, kind, title or site, from `createRecord` and `saveIdentifiers` (tested, for a current and a former number; `updateRecord` saves numbers through the same `saveIdentifierSet`). So all it says is that some record of the organization holds or held that number. (2) **Counters:** auto-number counters (`identifier_counters`, D-061) are per organization and type, shared by all its sites, so a person allocating the next number can tell from a gap that numbers were given out elsewhere. Accepted: the numbers are the organization's own series (D-019 keeps each organization's ID numbers separate, not each site's; D-074 keeps ID numbers with the home organization), and a site that wants its own series uses its own identifier type.
- **Amendment (2026-11-24, manual session, S-020).** One more accepted one-bit signal: `inherits_from_hidden` when publishing a record, or editing or restoring a published one, would put out values inherited from a record the person can't see (D-076's S-020 amendment). It says only that such a record is involved, never which, how many or what; the person already sees the record's parent or represented record ID in the record itself.

## D-076 — The stored workflow state: a column on records changed only as a version of the record, the publish check in the publishing transaction, published records kept publishable, and public output that reads the published list from the database (2026-11-24, autopilot T-034b, T-034c)
Builds D-075's states and moves into storage and the service. Migration `0012_workflow_states.sql` (0010 is T-037's sessions, 0011 T-041's roles; the runner allows gaps), `packages/db/src/workflow.ts`, and the four actions in `packages/service/src/service.ts`.
- **Where the state lives.** `records.workflow_state` (CHECK on D-075's four names, `draft` for every existing and new record), with a partial index on published records. *Rejected:* a separate state table (a second row to keep in step with the record, and its own history to build); a state per site now (D-075: per-site showing goes beside the state later, as (record, site) rows).
- **Every change of state is a version of the record.** `record_versions.action` gains `submit_for_review`, `return_to_draft`, `publish` and `withdraw`, and each version keeps `workflow_state`, the state once it was written, **filled in by the database from the row** (a writer's value is overwritten). History therefore says who published or withdrew a record, when and why, with the rest of its history (`readVersions` returns `action` and `workflowState`; `changedFields` of a workflow version is `["workflowState"]`). The database enforces, for every writer and in replica mode (every trigger stays ALWAYS): version 1 is a `save` of a `draft`; a `save` or `delete` never changes the state (so saving, restoring and identifier changes leave it alone); a workflow version changes nothing but the state and only along D-075's moves for its action (`workflow_move_allowed`, the same table as core's, compared by a test); a `delete` version is refused while the record is published (`record_versions_delete_published`); and the COMMIT check (`records_have_version`) compares the state too. A change of state bumps the record's version like any write, so an editor holding an older version gets `version_conflict`. *Rejected:* a workflow log beside the versions (two histories to read and keep in step); letting a save change the state (a direct writer could publish with an ordinary save).
- **The service's actions.** `submitForReview`, `returnToDraft`, `publish`, `withdraw` (context, record ID, `{ expectedVersion, reason? }`), each in one transaction under the record's row lock (`changeWorkflowState`, the same `FOR NO KEY UPDATE` an edit takes, so an edit and a change of state never interleave). `publish` writes the change and then runs `publishCheckIssues` in the same transaction; any issue rolls it back. Not-allowed moves give core's `action_not_allowed`/`transition_not_allowed`; another organization's record is `unknown_record`, exactly as a missing one.
- **The publish check reads everything itself** (D-054): the record's ancestry and represented records (one recursive statement following `parent_id` and `represents_id`), the records its own and its ancestors' values link to with their ancestry, all their templates (shared `definition_lock`, so a new template version waits for the publish or is seen by it), the identifier types (`FOR SHARE`) and the record's and ancestors' identifiers, never the whole catalog and never anything from a caller (at most 10,000 records, else `too_many_records`). It is `effectivePublishIssues` with an accession register. **Accessions are not stored yet (T-018b), so the register holds none and holds nothing back**; its `today` is the database's UTC day, which changes nothing while there are no accessions. T-034e must replace it with the organization's local day (D-056) before accessions are stored.
- **A published record stays publishable.** An edit, a restore or an identifier save of a published record runs the same check after writing, in the same transaction, and is refused (`published_record_check`, then the check's issues; fixed messages for a restore, D-065) rather than quietly withdrawing it. A published record can't be deleted (`published`; the database refuses its tombstone too): withdraw first.
- **Changes to the public output need the publish permission** (D-036, D-072's `records.publish`). `publish`, `withdraw`, and, on a published record, a non-empty `confirmTemplateMove` (updateRecord) or `confirmMadePublic` (restoreVersion) call `ServiceOptions.mayChangePublicOutput(tx, { organizationId, actor, recordId, change })` in the operation's transaction before anything is kept. This answers T-041's question: **confirming values public needs `records.publish` when the record is published** (it changes what the public sees at once); on any other record the edit permission is enough, because nothing goes public until someone with the publish permission publishes it. A plain edit of a published record needs only the edit permission (D-075; the mockup lets catalogers edit records, and Omeka S works the same way); John may want to change that. **The hook's default allows every actor**, matching every other operation on main until T-041 is merged; T-041's merge must replace the default with its `records.publish` check through `RecordAccess` (a record the actor can't view answered as `unknown_record`). A hook answer that isn't `undefined` or a refusal with issues is treated as a refusal (`not_permitted`). *Rejected:* a hook on the per-request `ServiceContext` (built by callers from a request path, so a caller could pass an allow-all); checking the permission on every edit of a published record (would stop catalogers fixing typos on published records, a museum-practice change for John).
- **Public output reads what is published from the database** (T-034c; replaces, for every caller outside core, D-016's caller-given `published` list, and closes the last part of T-010a). `readPublicRecord(db, organizationId, recordId, { baseUrl, mappings })` opens its own Repeatable Read, read-only transaction, so the state, records, templates, identifiers and the published list all come from one snapshot, and runs `effectiveRecord` → `publicView` → `publicOutput` with `published` = the published records among those it read. A record that isn't published, doesn't exist or is another organization's gets the identical answer `not_available`; any other issue is staff only (D-016, S-007). Links and place records that aren't published show nothing (D-045, D-048); values inherited from an unpublished ancestor may go out on a published child, never the ancestor's title or ID (D-016). Template changes after publishing are seen at the next read: a field made staff no longer goes out, and a mapping still naming it refuses the output. Any role with SELECT can run it; it writes nothing. `publicOutput` in core keeps its `published` option for tests and for this function. Staff search (D-066) is staff only and unchanged; public search and Subjects counts (T-034d) build on `readPublicRecord`.
- **What the writer can still do with direct SQL:** publish a record that fails the publish check, by a `publish` version along an allowed move (the database can't run core's checks); the public output still re-checks everything on reading (D-054), so it fails closed and shows the record as not available. It can't change a state without a version, by a save, along a move that doesn't exist, with a content change, start a record published, or delete a published record.
- **Left for later:** T-034d (public counts), T-034e (the organization's local day, re-publishing when a `closed_until` date passes); a staff list of published records that no longer pass the check (an ancestor's or template's change after publishing makes `readPublicRecord` refuse them; nothing re-checks them at that moment); per-site showing (Phase 5).
- Sources: PostgreSQL 16 docs `sql-set-transaction.html` (SET TRANSACTION before the first query of a transaction), `queries-with.html` (recursive CTEs, UNION stopping at repeated rows), `ddl-alter.html` (a column added with a default fills existing rows without rewriting them), and those D-063 and D-067 cite; read 2026-11-24.
- **John (2026-11-24): edits to a published record go live right away** with the edit permission, as in Omeka S, as long as the record still passes the publish check. Known consequences (from the review): an edit can put new public values live, including text typed into a public field, move a record under a parent with different inherited values, or add public identifier numbers; editing an unpublished ancestor changes a published child's public output, and if the child then fails the check it drops off the public sites (fails closed).
- **Open for the template and identifier-type save service (T-042 / T-047):** a template or identifier-type change that makes a field or number public publishes it at once on every published record. That service must list the published records affected and require `records.publish`.
- **Amendment (2026-11-24, manual session, S-020): the publisher must see what goes out.** `records.publish` was checked on the record only, but its public output includes public values inherited from ancestors (and, for a digital asset, from the record it represents and its ancestry), including unpublished ones outside the publisher's scope. Now `publish` also requires `records.view` on **every record a public value is inherited from, and on the record a digital asset represents** (`publicValueSources` in `packages/db/src/workflow.ts`, from the same effective record the publish check computes: entries not the record's own, in its template, whose effective visibility is public). Checked in the publishing transaction after the publish check: the sources are locked `FOR SHARE` in ID order and computed again (a change meanwhile gives `retry`), so none moves to a site or unit the publisher can't see before COMMIT; locks are taken upward only (a record, then what it inherits from), so they can't form a cycle. **Edits and restores of a published record** compute the sources before the write and refuse (`inherits_from_hidden`) any source added by the write that the person can't see (for example moving a published file from a series under one fonds to a series under another fonds above the person's scope); sources that were already there stay allowed, so a scoped cataloger can still fix a typo in a published record whose fonds they can't see. *Rejected:* requiring view on every ancestor whatever it contributes (a scoped reviewer could then publish nothing under a fonds they can't see, even when nothing is inherited); stopping inheritance at a scope or site boundary (it would change published output for everyone, depending on who looks). The refusal names no record (fixed text, tested); that some value would come from a record the person can't see is a one-bit signal, added to D-074's accepted list. Edits of an *ancestor* that change a published child's output stay as John decided above (the editor sees the ancestor they edit). Tests (service `workflow.test.ts`): a reviewer scoped to a series refused publishing it under a fonds with an inherited public value (state stays draft, nothing public), allowed when the fonds contributes nothing; a reviewer scoped to two series refused moving a published file from one to the other, allowed a plain rename; both fail without the check.
  - **Second review (2026-11-24).** Two holes closed. (1) *Edits of records above a published one.* A scoped editor could still put a hidden ancestor's value out by editing an unpublished unit they can see: with fonds F (public inherited provenance) above series S (its own provenance) above published file X, dropping S's provenance made X show F's. Now every edit or restore of a record lists the records below it and the scans of them (`recordDependents`); unless the person is a system manager, nothing is published among them, or none of the records a new source could be (the record, everything above it before and after the write, everything below it) is hidden from the person (`recordClosureIds`, `hiddenAmong`), the published dependents' pairs are read before the write and compared after it, and any new pair whose source the person can't see refuses the write (`inherits_from_hidden`, fixed text). A plain cataloger is refused the same way. Deleting is not covered because a record with children or scans can't be deleted (D-067). (2) *Pairs, not IDs.* `publicValueSources` now returns (field, source) pairs and the checks compare pairs, so a hidden fonds that already gave one field can't start giving another. Tests: the review's repro for a reviewer and for a cataloger (and the same edit allowed to someone who sees the fonds), the second-field variant, and unrelated edits (a new title, a new own value) still passing; all three refusal tests fail without the check. Cost: an edit of a unit with many published records below it reads each one's pairs twice, but only when something hidden is involved.
  - **Final review (2026-11-24): locks, so a publication and an edit above it can't pass each other.** The checks above read without locks, so an edit of S that lets a hidden value down (here: S's template no longer stops it) could read file X as unpublished while another transaction published X, which saw nothing from the hidden fonds yet; both committed and X showed the fonds's value (reproduced for an unscoped site manager on two pools; a scoped person was already kept apart by the arrangement lock). Now, for anyone but a system manager (who sees every record): an edit or restore first locks every record below it and every scan of them `FOR SHARE` (`lockBelow`, one statement in ID order, after the arrangement lock and before its own row), then its own row, then every record above it `FOR SHARE` (`lockAbove`, which reads the set again and answers `retry` if it moved), and after its row lock reads the records below again (`retry` if one moved in unlocked). Publishing, after its row lock, locks the record's whole chain the same way before reading its pairs. **The order is always: records below, the record, records above**, so two transactions on a record and its parent, or on a record and a publication below it, wait for each other instead of deadlocking or passing. Publishing a record waits while an edit above it holds it `FOR SHARE`, and then reads the edit's result. Tests: the reviewer's race pinned (the edit paused after its write on one pool, the publication on another must still be waiting after 1.5 s, then is refused with `inherits_from_hidden`, and the file stays unpublished without the fonds's value; it fails with the locks removed), and six rounds of a series edit, a fonds edit, a file publication and a file edit at once by two unscoped site managers, with `pg_stat_database.deadlocks` unchanged. *Cost (L1):* an edit by anyone but a system manager locks every record below it in one statement, and, only when a record they can't see is involved, compares every published record below it; above `MAX_WATCHED_DEPENDENTS` (500) such records the edit is refused (`too_many_published_below`, fixed text, naming nothing) and a system manager makes it. The hidden-record check reads each record's place once (`hiddenAmong`); a set-based version is a later optimisation.
  - **Amendment (2026-11-24, S-028): the dependents check only for edits that can change what is inherited.** The S-027 review measured a whole-site manager's title change on a unit with 250 published below at 4.2 s and 6,841 statements, locking the whole subtree. Now:
    - *The rule.* `changesInheritance(before, after)` (core, `record-compare.ts`) is true unless every difference between the stored record and the edit (or the version restored) is in `INHERITANCE_INERT_PROPERTIES`: `title`, `recordTypeKey`, `mediaType`, `agentType`, `coordinates`. Everything else counts, including anything added to records later: `fields` (every key's presence and value, since an empty value stops inheritance), `templateKey`, `parentId`, `level`, `represents`, `siteId` (which changes no (field, source) pair but changes who can see the record) and `kind`/`id`/`organizationId` (never change; a pair that can't be compared counts). `effectiveRecord` reads `title` only into chain steps and staff messages and never reads the others listed; linked records count by kind only. An edit that also saves identifier numbers (`updateRecord`'s `identifiers`) always counts: numbers decide whether an inherited identifier value is public, though only for pairs whose source is the edited record, which the editor sees (`saveIdentifiers` on its own never compared dependents, for that reason). Tested: core enumerates every property of every kind (set, changed, removed, added) against a table that must classify each one (`RECORD_PROPERTIES` is exported for it); the service test checks, from another connection while the edit is paused after its write, that a title change locks nothing below and that a field's value, a field removed or added, the template and the parent each lock every record below; and the same for restores.
    - *Skipped when it can't matter.* An edit or restore that doesn't change inheritance takes no below-locks (`lockBelow`) and compares nothing (`watchDependents`). The decision is made before the row lock from a look that locks no row (`belowAtStake`: `RecordAccess.allowsUnlocked` and the stored record) and made again under the row lock from the locked record; the record's version is checked between the two (another version is a `version_conflict` or a `retry`, as before), so both answers are the same, and if the locked answer ever needs the locks the first look didn't take, the operation starts again (`retry`). `lockAbove`, the published record's own check (`hiddenSourcesRefusal`) and the publication side are unchanged, so the order stays below, record, above. A skipped edit can't race a publication below it: the publication waits on the edited row (it locks its chain `FOR SHARE`) and then reads the same (field, source) pairs as before the edit.
    - *Below-locks after the permission and version checks.* The first look also asks whether the write will be refused anyway (no such record, not at the version the person saw, or not permitted: `allowsUnlocked` answers as `onRecord` does, tested for every record of a mixed tree and five kinds of actor), and then takes no below-locks; the refusal itself still comes from the checks under the row lock, unchanged. The look locks no row on purpose: `onRecord` for someone with scoped grants locks the records a scan stands for `FOR SHARE`, and taking that before the below-locks would put an "above" lock first (a deadlock with an edit of that record, which locks below it first).
    - *The cap.* `too_many_published_below` (500) now applies only to edits that change inheritance: a title change with 501 published below by a manager who can't see the fonds is saved; a change of an inherited value is still refused (naming nothing) and a system manager makes it (tested).
    - *`hiddenAmong` set-based.* One recursive statement reads the places of all the records asked about and everything above them (parents of units, represented records of scans), and `placesHolding` (db, `permissions.ts`) works out `unitsHolding`'s answer for each in memory, step for step, then locks the records a scan's chain passes through `FOR SHARE` in one statement in ID order, the rows `unitsHolding` locked one at a time (for scoped grants only, under the arrangement lock, so the read-then-lock order changes nothing). Tested against the old one-by-one reading on a tree with units and scans on two sites, a scan of a scan, a chain of ten scans (past `unitsHolding`'s eight hops), a unit on no site and IDs that don't exist or aren't IDs, for a scoped cataloger, a reviewer scoped on two sites, an every-site cataloger, a whole-site manager and a system manager: the same answers and the same rows locked, in at most three statements.
    - *Measured* (`packages/service/src/dependents.test.ts`, run with `OCULUS_S028_BELOW=250 … --silent=false`; asserts statement counts only; local throwaway PostgreSQL 16, milliseconds for information): a whole-site manager's title change on a series with 250 published files below and a fonds he can't see, **5,538 statements / about 3.0 s before, 33 statements / about 25 ms after** (82 → 33 with 2 below; the count no longer grows with what is below); a scoped cataloger's, **5,791 → 36**. A change of an inherited value still compares every published record below: 5,538 → 5,288 (whole-site) and 5,791 → 5,291 (scoped), the drop being `hiddenAmong`; about 21 statements per published record remain (each one's pairs read before and after the write, and its chain locked), a cost only such edits pay.
- **Amendment (2026-11-24, manual session, S-019).** `readPublicRecord` now takes the public site and reads through `public_record_snapshot` only: a record is public on a site when it is published and the site is its home site (records on no site never are), links resolve only to records public on that site, and public sites connect with a role that can read no table. See D-080.

## D-077 — Two-step sign-in: TOTP sealed with `OCULUS_SECRET_KEY`, a backward hash chain the database checks so promotion needs a real second step, recovery codes checked by the database, codes throttled per person (TOTP only), per session and per address with alerts, a one-time enrolment link after a reset, the otpauth link only (no QR dependency) (2026-11-24, autopilot T-038)
Builds D-052's two-step part on D-068 and D-069. Pure rules in `packages/core/src/totp.ts`; the key and sealing in `packages/db/src/secret-key.ts`; the verifier in `packages/db/src/second-factor.ts`; passes in `second-step.ts`; tables and guards in `packages/db/migrations/0014_second_factors.sql` (0012 is main's workflow states, 0011 and 0013 T-041's). No new dependency: `node:crypto` only. Sources: RFC 6238 (§4 T0/X, §5.2 one step of delay and no second use, Appendix B vectors) and RFC 4226 (§5.3 truncation, R6 160-bit secrets, §7.3 throttling, Appendix D vectors), both fetched from rfc-editor.org 2026-11-24; RFC 4648 §6 (base32); RFC 5869 (HKDF); RFC 1760 (S/KEY, the backward hash chain); NIST SP 800-38D (GCM, 96-bit random nonces); OWASP ASVS 5.0 6.3.1, 6.5.1–6.5.5, 7.5.1; PostgreSQL 16 docs `functions-info.html` (`pg_current_xact_id`), `functions-binarystring.html` (`sha256`), `runtime-config-logging.html` (parameter logging).
- **Codes** (D-052 as decided): HMAC-SHA-1, 6 digits, 30 s from the epoch, steps as `bigint`; one step either side, by **the database's clock** (`totp_step_now()`, the clock sessions already use); every step of the window computed and compared with `timingSafeEqual`, no early exit; the last accepted step is stored per factor and a code for it or an earlier step is never accepted (in code, and again in the trigger, so two connections can't both use one code). Tested against RFC 4226's ten HOTP values and RFC 6238's six SHA-1 vectors (8 digits), including the 2603 one.
- **Set-up at first sign-in, required for everyone** (D-052, John). A session waiting for its second step whose person has no active factor gets `enrolment_required` from `completeSecondStep`; `startEnrolment` makes a 20-byte secret and its hash chain, stores it sealed as a *pending* factor bound to that waiting session, and returns it once as base32 and an `otpauth://totp/<issuer>:<address>?secret=…&issuer=…&algorithm=SHA1&digits=6&period=30` link (issuer "Oculus" unless the caller names one). `confirmEnrolment` (same waiting session only) checks a code, then in **one transaction** activates the factor, stores 10 recovery codes and promotes the session; the codes are returned once. Restarting replaces an unconfirmed factor (at most 5 starts per waiting session; starts for one person are serialised on the user row, so two at once leave one pending factor and count nothing). One live factor per person (a unique index), so an active factor can't be replaced by starting again; it can only be reset by a system admin. `choose_organization` (several memberships) commits nothing, so the same code is sent again with the organization.
- **QR codes: not here.** The verifier returns only the `otpauth://` link; T-045's sign-in screen renders it as a QR code next to the base32 key. That keeps a rendering dependency out of the server packages; T-045 chooses the encoder (for example `uqr`, MIT, no dependencies) and runs the gate's license check on it. *Rejected:* adding a QR encoder to `@oculus/db` now (the server never needs an image; the link is the whole contract).
- **`OCULUS_SECRET_KEY`.** At least 32 random bytes as base64url, base64 or hex (hex wins when the text is all hex digits and 64+ long); refused as `secret_key_missing`, `secret_key_malformed` or `secret_key_weak` (under 32 bytes, or fewer than 16 different byte values) by `loadSecretKeyring`, whose errors never contain the value. **T-045/T-051 must call it at startup and refuse to start on an error**; every two-step function also refuses (TypeError) anything but a keyring it made. Keys: HKDF-SHA-256, salt `oculus/OCULUS_SECRET_KEY/v1`, info `totp secret encryption` (AES-256 key) and `key id` (8-byte public fingerprint); later uses of the variable take their own info strings. Sealed secret: `0x01 ‖ key id ‖ 12-byte random nonce ‖ 16-byte tag ‖ ciphertext`, with the factor ID and user ID as additional data, so a box copied to another row or person doesn't open. Key bytes live only in a module-private WeakMap; the keyring object holds the fingerprint alone. **Rotation:** new key in `OCULUS_SECRET_KEY`, old ones in `OCULUS_SECRET_KEY_PREVIOUS` (comma-separated), run `resealSecondFactorSecrets` (as the writer) until `resealed` is 0, then drop the old key. A secret no loaded key opens gives `second_step_unavailable` (the attempt is given back to the throttle): the person uses a recovery code, or a system admin resets them. *Rejected:* a key in the database (a dump would carry it), a passphrase-derived key (scrypt at every start for no gain over random bytes).
- **Recovery codes** (D-052): 10 per set, 120 bits as 24 Crockford base32 characters shown in six groups of four; typed in either case with spaces or dashes, and O→0, I/L→1. Stored as SHA-256 of the canonical text (`PRIMARY KEY (user_id, code_hash)`). A use is a pass (below), so it is logged. `regenerateRecoveryCodes` makes a new set (the database supersedes the old one in the same transaction) and needs **the password and a code entered again in the last 10 minutes** (`reauthentication_required`; review item 2) plus a code or current recovery code now, counted per session (5 wrong sign that session out) and per address, so a stolen session cookie alone can neither replace the codes nor move the person's counter. `secondFactorStatus` reports whether it is set up, how many codes and TOTP passes are left.
- **Throttling** (RFC 4226 §7.3, ASVS 6.3.1; review item 1). `second_step_throttles` holds three kinds of counter, all counted up front in one transaction locking the rows in key order, an attempt during a wait not counted (D-068):
  - *per person* (`user:<id>`, core's `SECOND_STEP_THROTTLE`, D-068's account rule exactly: 5 free, waits doubling from 1 s to **15 minutes**, forgotten after a day), for **TOTP codes only**. Recovery codes (120 bits) skip it, so someone who has the password and guesses TOTP codes can make the person wait for TOTP but can never keep them out: D-052's "no lockout" holds.
  - *per session* (`session:<id>`): after **5 wrong codes** (recovery codes included) a waiting session is deleted (`too_many_attempts`, the password is needed again) and a full one is signed out (re-entry, new recovery codes, an admin's reset).
  - *per client address* (core's `SOURCE_THROTTLE` and keys: 20 free, waits to 15 minutes), every code including recovery codes. `completeSecondStep`, `confirmEnrolment`, `regenerateRecoveryCodes` and `resetSecondFactor` therefore take `source` like `reauthenticate`.

  A right code clears the person's and the session's counters and takes one off the address's; an admin reset clears the person's. Someone with the password guessing all year gets about 96 tries a day once waiting: with three codes valid of a million, about a 1 in 3,500 chance a day and about 10% a year. So **every wait is recorded**: when the person's counter starts a wait and every 10 wrong codes after, a row in `second_step_alerts` (user, failure count, time; insert only). **T-040 and T-048 must tell the person and the system admins from these rows; that notice is required before deployment** (roadmap notes added). Only a password holder or a session holder can move the person's counter (the latter only after a recent re-entry, which itself needs the password). Malformed input is `invalid_request`, not counted. **A lost race is not a wrong code** (review item 6): a right code whose pass the database refused because another connection used it first gives its attempt back (tested with the factor row pinned so both pass the code check first). The cleanup command deletes day-old counters and those of ended sessions.
- **Bound in the database** (D-069's requirement). The database can't check a TOTP code (the secret is sealed with a key it never sees), so it checks a proof that needs the secret: a **backward hash chain** (S/KEY, RFC 1760; review item 3). At set-up the application computes `L_0 = HMAC-SHA-256(secret, "oculus second step chain v1" ‖ factor)` and `L_{k+1} = SHA-256(L_k)`, and the factor stores `L_N` (`chain_length`, 20,000 for new factors: about 5 years at 10 passes a day, at most about 50 ms of hashing per pass) as its anchor. A TOTP pass row (`second_step_passes`) carries the next link back, which must hash to the stored anchor, and **that link becomes the new anchor**: the pass's writer chooses nothing. The trigger moves the anchor, generation and last step together (only to a later step within two of the clock's, never past `chain_length`, which never changes), and clears the proof before the row is written. A recovery-code pass carries the code, which the trigger hashes and marks used, then clears. A pass is valid only in the transaction that wrote it (`created_xact = pg_current_xact_id()` and `created_at >= now()`, so a restored row can't match a future transaction ID), for its purpose and session. Then: promotion needs a `sign_in` pass for that very session and user; a new `password_at` needs a `reauth` pass for that session; new recovery codes need the factor confirmed in this transaction or a `recovery_codes` pass; revoking an active factor and making an enrolment token need an active system admin's `admin_reset` pass for that person; a switch copy must carry its live source's `second_step_pass`. `sessions.second_step_pass` and `reauth_pass` record them (`full` ⇔ a pass, a CHECK). Passes are append-only (the writer has INSERT only; triggers refuse UPDATE and TRUNCATE for anyone who can't disable triggers). Existing sessions were deleted by 0014 (nothing is deployed). In process, `promoteSession` still needs `passedSecondStep`'s proof, which now carries the pass material and writes the pass inside its own transaction; only `second-factor.ts` makes proofs, and it is on the import-scan test's list.
  - *Why a backward chain* (review item 3): the first version stored `sha256(r_{g+1})` chosen by the pass's writer, so one leaked unused link plus the writer role gave permanent control. Now a leaked link not yet used gives at most the passes between it and the current anchor, and nothing beyond it. Links are never stored, only sent once in the pass's INSERT; still, **the database server must not log statement parameters** (`log_statement` not `all` or `mod`, `log_min_duration_statement` and `log_parameter_max_length` without bound values, `log_parameter_max_length_on_error` = 0, as D-068 already asks for errors), since a link, a recovery code or an enrolment token is a bearer value until used. T-051 checks these settings.
  - *No re-seeding.* When the chain is used up the factor takes no more TOTP passes (`second_step_unavailable`): the person signs in with a recovery code and a system admin resets them (`totpPassesLeft` lets the screens warn long before). *Rejected:* re-seeding on a successful code, because the new chain's anchor would again be chosen by whoever writes the pass, which brings back the leaked-link problem at every re-seed.
- **What the writer role can't do any more with direct SQL** (tested as a role made by `grantWriterRole`, with `checkWriterConnection` returning `[]`): promote a waiting session by UPDATE (no pass, or another session's or person's); write a pass with a made-up link or recovery code; move the chain to an anchor of its choosing, lengthen it, or move the last step; revoke an active factor or start a second one beside it; add recovery codes or mark one used without presenting it; make an enrolment token; record a recent password entry without a pass; copy a full session with a pass that isn't its source's; change or delete passes, or delete factors. In replica mode too (all 0014 triggers are ALWAYS).
- **What it still can**, precisely (beside D-067–D-069): **take over an account that has never set up its second step and was never reset** (a new account): it can insert a waiting session for anyone (0010 allows that; the database can't check a password) and then a pending factor with a chain of its own, which is exactly the set-up D-052 authorises with the password alone. A system admin's reset evicts it for good: from then on only the emailed token sets up (see below). **Clone or re-token a live full session** of someone signed in (switching and `rotateSession` legitimately make new tokens without a code), so it can ride along a session while it lives; set any password hash (D-068); revoke a person's unconfirmed set-up; replace a factor's sealed secret with garbage (TOTP then fails as `second_step_unavailable`; recovery codes still work); delete or rewrite the wrong-code counters and insert fake alerts. Anyone with the writer role **and** `OCULUS_SECRET_KEY` can open every secret and compute codes and links, so the two must never be stored together.
- **Re-entering the password** (`reauthenticate`, moved from sessions.ts because it now needs the verifier) takes the password, the client address and a code or recovery code: the password first (signIn's throttle and replies), then the code, then one transaction that locks the session row, writes the `reauth` pass and sets the session's new token and `password_at`, under the same "hash and `sessions_valid_after` unchanged" conditions as before. **Lock order everywhere: user rows, then session rows, then factor rows.** Re-entry and new recovery codes lock their session row before the pass locks the factor row (second review: without it a re-entry held the factor row while a reset held the session row, a deadlock, reproduced by a pinned test that now passes). They don't lock the user row, which would also hold off a password change that must win (T-037's R1). `endUserSessions` and every later "recently authenticated" check therefore mean password **and** second step.
- **A system admin's reset** (`resetSecondFactor(db, keys, adminToken, userId, { code | recoveryCode, source }, deliver)`): the admin's own full session, a system admin, re-authenticated in the last 10 minutes, **and** a code of their own now (the database needs the admin's pass in the same transaction). Then, in one transaction: **the user rows first, in ID order** (the target's `FOR NO KEY UPDATE`, the admin's `FOR SHARE`), then the target's session rows, then the pass (the admin's factor row), then every session of the target ends (`endAllSessionsForUser`), their factor is revoked (a trigger also ends sessions), a one-time **enrolment token** is stored (32 random bytes, SHA-256 only, 3 days) and their counter cleared (review item 4: the first version locked the admin's factor before the target's user row and could deadlock with a promotion; a test pins that interleaving and checks `pg_stat_database.deadlocks` is unchanged, and fails with the old order). After the commit the token goes to the person's address through `deliver` (an `EnrolmentDelivery`, T-040 makes it email; the reply says `delivered`, and resetting again sends a new one). **After a reset, every `startEnrolment` needs that token** (`enrolment_token_required`; review item 5, tightened by the second review). Every reset makes a token, whether or not the person had a factor in use, and from then until a factor is confirmed the database refuses a pending factor unless the person's **newest** unexpired token was presented (checked against its hash) **in the same transaction**; the token can be presented again for later starts, from any waiting session, until a factor is confirmed or it expires (`MAX_ENROLMENT_STARTS` per waiting session still applies), and a newer reset replaces it. *Rejected* (second review): letting later starts in the same waiting session skip the token. It was keyed on a flag any factor row of that session carried, so the writer could revoke the person's pending set-up and insert its own with the same session ID, reuse an old session ID after a second reset, or set the flag on its own factor with a moving UPDATE; all three are now tested as the writer role and refused. Also: a pending set-up can be revoked, after a reset, only by a start presenting the token or by another reset. A *pass* that confirms a pending factor must come from the waiting session that started it; that rule binds passes only. A factor can also be confirmed by a direct UPDATE that moves its chain, but only with that factor's next link, so only whoever made the factor (which after a reset needed the token) or holds its secret can do it. **"Newest token" and "confirmed since" are decided by order, not time** (third review): every token and every confirmation takes a number from the sequence `second_step_order` (through the owner's `second_step_next_order()`, so the writer needs no sequence privilege; calling it only skips numbers), so a clock stepping back can't drop the requirement (tested), and `pg_dump` keeps the sequence. **The token has no foreign key to the pass that made it** (checked by the insert trigger only), so deleting that pass, or by cascade the admin's account, can't take the token and the requirement away (tested). So the password alone, which the writer role could set (D-068), is no longer enough to re-enrol someone who was reset. `startEnrolment` makes every refusal (a factor in use, too many starts, no valid token) with one read before it computes the chain, so a refused start costs no hashing. Tokens follow D-068's secrecy rule (only in the message to the person). An admin may reset their own; a person with no factor in use just gets a new token. T-039's password reset must not undo this: a password reset does not lift the token requirement (roadmap note).
- **For T-041, T-048 and anything after: `system_admin` must stay un-writable by the writer role** (review item 7). The reset path trusts "an active system admin's pass"; if a later task let the writer make system admins (today `system_admin` is fixed by 0009's identity trigger and `CHECK (system_admin = bootstrap)`), a writer could make itself an admin, reset anyone and, with a delivered token, take the account. Any change to how system admins are made needs its own review of this path.
- **The secret never leaves the package** except once from `startEnrolment`: it is opened for one check and wiped (`fill(0)`); results never hold it; the stored row holds only the sealed box. Database errors reach `onError` only as a `SecondStepDatabaseError` with the SQLSTATE and constraint (a failed statement may have carried a chain link, recovery code or enrolment token, and pg's `where`/`detail` can repeat parameters, D-068). Tested with a statement timeout.
- **Writer grants** (`SECOND_FACTOR_TABLE_PRIVILEGES` in `WRITER_PRIVILEGES`): `second_factors` INSERT, UPDATE (also for the pass trigger's `FOR UPDATE`); `second_step_passes` INSERT; `second_step_recovery_codes` INSERT, UPDATE; `second_step_throttles` INSERT, UPDATE, DELETE; `second_step_alerts` INSERT; `second_step_enrolment_tokens` INSERT, UPDATE. Nothing deletes a factor, pass, recovery code, token or alert.
- **Tests changed and why.** `sessions.test.ts` promoted sessions with `passedSecondStep(sessionId, userId)` alone, which T-037 allowed only until this task; promotion now needs a real pass, so its helpers give each test person a factor with a pool of recovery codes (`second-factor.fixtures.ts`, as the owner with the guard triggers off inside one transaction; short chains so tests stay fast) and its proofs carry one code each; `reauthenticate` calls add a recovery code. Every assertion is unchanged. `migrate.test.ts` lists the new tables.
- **Left for later:** a person replacing their own authenticator (today: an admin reset); WebAuthn (D-052); the wrong-code notice from `second_step_alerts` (T-040/T-048, required before deployment); the HTTP routes and screens (T-045, which must call `loadSecretKeyring` at startup); an event log of sign-ins and resets beyond the passes and alerts tables.
- **Amendment (2026-11-24, manual session, S-018).** `reauthenticate`'s password is no longer counted on the sign-in account counter: it has its own `reauth:<user ID>` counter (account rule) beside the address counter, so strangers typing the person's address at the sign-in form can't hold a signed-in person off re-entry. Details in D-068's S-018 amendment.

## D-078 — Password reset links: one frozen reply sent before anything about the account is read, a 256-bit single-use token living 30 minutes, a newer link revoking the older, use that sets the password and ends every session in one transaction without signing in, and a system admin's link (2026-11-24, T-039)
Builds D-052's "resetting a password ends all of its sessions" and D-068's token secrecy rule on D-069's sessions. Pure rules in `packages/core/src/password-reset.ts`; data access in `packages/db/src/password-resets.ts`; the table in `packages/db/migrations/0015_password_resets.sql` (after T-041's 0013 and T-038's 0014, whose `second_factors` it reads). No new dependency (`node:crypto` only). Checked against OWASP ASVS 5.0 V6 (6.3.8, 6.4.1–6.4.3), V7.4.2 and the OWASP Forgot Password cheat sheet.
- **Asking (`requestPasswordReset`): the same reply, and the same work before it, for every address.** The request's shape is checked first (`invalid_request` for non-text, NUL, broken UTF-16 or more than 1,024 characters: nothing about any account, nothing counted). Then the reset counters (below) count it. Then the reply is **the one frozen `RESET_REQUESTED`** ("If that address belongs to an account that can sign in, a link … is on its way"), returned **before anything about the account is read**: looking the address up, storing the link and sending it all run afterwards, in the background. So an account, no account, a malformed address, a disabled account and one with no organization get the same object after the same statements (tested: `toBe` the same reply, and median times within a factor of 2 plus 1 ms). *Rejected:* awaiting the send (a real mail send takes hundreds of milliseconds, so the reply time would say whether an account exists); padding the reply to a fixed time (it would have to cover the 30-second send limit); answering differently for a disabled account (D-068 answers sign-in the same way for it).
  - The background work never throws: database failures and programming errors go to `onError`, and `onSettled(outcome)` hears what became of it (`sent`, `not_sent`, `delivery_failed`, `delivery_failed_may_be_pending`, `busy`, `database_unavailable`) for the server's log only; neither ever gets the token or the address. At most `MAX_RESET_WORK` (32) requests are *worked on* (database and send) at once in a process. **A slot is taken only when the work starts, after the random wait** (second review, M1: taking it before the wait let 32 requests from one IPv6 /48 fill the cap in 48 ms and keep a real member's link back for the whole wait; about 6 requests a second kept it full). Waiting requests are bounded separately (`MAX_WAITING_RESETS`, 10,000 timers). Work that finds every slot taken waits again, a random 0.5 to 2 s, up to `RESET_WORK_RETRIES` (5) times, and only then is dropped as `busy`, reported to `onError` with a fixed message so the log shows it loudly; a full waiting bound is dropped at once the same way. So a flood can't pile up sends or connections, and filling the cap needs 32 sends *in progress* at once, not 32 timers (tested: 40 requests waiting don't hold back a member's request, and work behind a full cap goes ahead when slots free up).
  - **The background work starts after a random wait** (`backgroundDelayMs`, 1 to 10 seconds by default, chosen with `crypto.randomInt`; 0 to 60 seconds allowed; tests pass 0), and for an address with no usable account it runs **dummy work of the same shape**: a locking read of `users` and an `UPDATE` of `password_resets` on an ID no account has, as storing a link does (only the INSERT is missing). The independent review (L1) found that without these the *next* request's latency told an existing account from an unknown one 72% of the time in one try, because the background transaction and send ran on the shared pool right after the reply. *What remains:* the INSERT, the send itself (outside the pool) and a busier pool for the few seconds the work takes, spread at random over 1–10 s and over everyone else's requests; telling them apart would need many requests for one address, which its counter allows 3 of before a wait. A measurement test was not added: with the random wait it can only fail by chance, and without it the test measures the old design; the reply's own timing stays tested. The cap counts only work in progress (database and send), so the wait doesn't lower the throughput: a send taking about a second still allows about 30 links a second per process.
- **Reset counters: D-068's throttle with rules and keys of its own.** In `sign_in_throttles` (so the existing grants, cleanup and locking in key order apply), keyed `reset:` plus the sign-in key: per address typed (SHA-256 of the lower-cased text, whether or not an account has it) 3 free, then waits of 1, 2, 4 … minutes up to 1 hour, forgotten after 24 hours without a request; per client address (IPv4, IPv6 /64) 10 free, then 30 s, 1, 2 … minutes up to 1 hour, forgotten after an hour; and for IPv6 also **per /48** (`reset:source:v6:…::/48`, a site's usual allocation, RFC 6177) 30 free, then the same waits (second review, M1), so one site's 65,536 /64s don't each get 10 free requests. The key CHECK allows that form for reset counters only, and the cleanup forgets it after its own rule's hour (tested). **Every request counts** (each may send an email), and a throttled request is answered `throttled` with `retryAfterSeconds`, which depends only on what was typed and where from, never on the account. Separate counters, so asking for links never makes anyone wait to sign in, and sign-in failures never block a reset (tested). 0015 widens the key CHECK to allow the prefix. A successful reset clears the address's sign-in **and** reset counters, and a system admin's `clearSignInThrottle` now clears both too (review L4), so someone asking for links in a person's name can't keep them from getting one for longer than until an admin steps in. As at sign-in (D-068), clients whose address can't be read share one `source:other` bucket (here `reset:source:other`): behind a proxy not configured as trusted, 10 requests from anyone make everyone wait; T-045's check that the client address is readable covers this route too. `deleteExpiredSignInThrottles` now forgets each kind of counter by its own rule's reset time (`reset:account:` and `account:` 24 hours, the address counters 1 hour), so the cleanup can't drift from the rules (review L6, tested). *Rejected:* counting requests on the sign-in counters (an attacker could then lock a person out of sign-in by asking for links).
- **The link.** A 32-byte random token as base64url in one canonical spelling (`isResetToken`, like sessions' and invitations'), sent once in the message to the account's own stored address, and stored only as SHA-256 (`token_hash`, unique; a plain hash suffices at 256 bits, as D-068). It lasts **30 minutes by default; a caller may choose 5 to 60 minutes** (`RESET_LIFE_BOUNDS`; the table's CHECK refuses anything else). One pending link per account (partial unique index): a new request locks the user row, revokes the pending one (`revoked_reason = 'superseded'`) and inserts the new one, stamped by the database's clock after the lock. It is committed before it is sent; `deliver(message, signal)` runs outside any transaction with a time limit (`deliveryTimeoutMs`, 30 s by default, at most 5 minutes), as `createInvitation` does (D-068); a send that throws, rejects or times out revokes the link (`delivery_failed`, tried twice). The message is `{ email, token, expiresAt, requestedBy: "self" | "system_admin" }`; T-040 writes the email and must follow D-068's secrecy rule. Whatever `deliver` throws is dropped unread (it might quote the message), never passed to `onError`.
- **A link dies when the account changes under it.** It is usable only while pending, unexpired, the account is active and can sign in (a system admin or a member of some organization), and it was made **after the account's `password_changed_at` and after its `sessions_valid_after`** (`password_reset_user_ok`). So a password change, "end all sessions", disabling (even if re-enabled later) or a change of system admin kills every link sent before it, with no list of links to revoke. Losing the last membership (for an account that isn't a system admin) **revokes** its pending links (`revoked_reason = 'no_membership'`, trigger `organization_members_end_resets`, ALWAYS, user row locked first), so inviting the person back doesn't bring an old link back to life (review L2, tested); losing one of two memberships leaves the link alone. 0015 also makes `password_changed_at` move only forward and never past the clock (`users_password_changed_at`, ALWAYS), so no writer can bring a link back by moving that time back (`sessions_valid_after` already only moves forward, D-069).
- **Using it (`completePasswordReset`).** It takes only the token and the new password, no account. Every reason a link can't be used (unknown, malformed, used, revoked, replaced, expired, the account changed) is the same `invalid_reset`. The new password is checked by D-068's rules in NFC (8+ characters, at most 1,024 bytes, not a common password, not mostly context words: the person's name and address, the names of all their organizations, and the built-in words), and issues come back under `password`; the link stays usable for another try. The hash is made outside any transaction (`busy` if the scrypt queue is full). Then **one transaction**: the user row `FOR NO KEY UPDATE` (the order every path that makes or ends sessions uses), the link claimed by `UPDATE … SET used_at = clock_timestamp() WHERE` still pending, unexpired and usable, the new hash and `password_changed_at = clock_timestamp()`, `endAllSessionsForUser` (deletes every session in every organization and stage and moves `sessions_valid_after`, D-069). **After the commit**, in a statement of its own, the address's sign-in and reset counters are deleted (D-068 asked a reset to clear the sign-in one). Not inside the transaction: `signIn`'s success transaction deletes that counter row and then updates the user row, the reverse of the reset's order, and the review (L3) showed the two deadlocking; tested pinned (a sign-in's success transaction held between its counter DELETE and its user UPDATE while a use runs: no deadlock by PostgreSQL's counter, and 3 in 3 rounds with the DELETE moved back inside). A failure of that DELETE only leaves a wait in place. The reply is `{ ok: true, value: { reset: true } }`: **no session and no token; the person signs in afresh**, with their second step (T-038). **A reset leaves the second factor alone**: it proves the mailbox, not the second factor, so a mailbox alone never gets anyone in; only a system manager's reset of two-step sign-in removes a factor (T-038).
- **At the same moment** (tested on two connections): two uses of one link, exactly one wins (the claim's row lock and its re-checked `WHERE`); a use racing a new request, the old link is settled either way and the new link always works, because the request stamps its link after taking the user row (looped); a use racing a sign-in leaves no live session from the old password, looped and also pinned between the sign-in's password check and its insert, by D-069's R1 check (the account's hash and `sessions_valid_after` unchanged under `FOR SHARE`).
- **For T-048 (the admin's Users page):** sending a reset link to an account with no second factor in use, or right after resetting its two-step sign-in, means whoever reads that mailbox can set a new password *and* set up two-step sign-in with the emailed enrolment token: the mailbox alone then gets the account. The screen must say so before sending, and suggest confirming the person's identity another way first.
- **A system admin's link (`sendPasswordReset`).** D-019 gives system managers "reset passwords". From their own full session (`adminToken`), read in the same transaction as the link is stored (`readActingSession`, also used now by `endUserSessions`), as a system admin with the password entered in the last 10 minutes (`reauthentication_required`, D-052). It sends the same kind of link to the account's own address (`requestedBy: "system_admin"`, the row's `requested_by`); the admin never sees the token. Since the admin already knows the account, the answers say what happened: `unknown_user`, `account_disabled`, `cannot_sign_in`, `delivery_failed`, `delivery_failed_may_be_pending`, `superseded` (the person's own request replaced it while it was sent) or `ok` with the expiry. Sending a link changes nothing until it is used; if the account may be in someone else's hands, `endUserSessions` ends its sessions at once. Not throttled (staff only). *Rejected:* an admin setting a password directly (they would know it); showing the admin a link to pass on (D-068: never a "copy link" button).
- **Guards (0015), all `ENABLE ALWAYS` (D-067), tested in replica mode.** A new link must be pending, made now (`created_at` between `now()` and `clock_timestamp()`), live 5 to 60 minutes (CHECK), for an account that can use it (the user row held `FOR SHARE` first, so a disable or "end all" under way finishes before the check reads it), and asked by the person (`requested_by` NULL) or an active system admin. A link is settled once: used (`used_at` now, only before `expires_at` by the statement's time, only while the account can use it) or revoked (now, with a reason), then never changes. `zzzz_password_resets_keep_identity` fixes its ID, user, token hash, requester and times. So **no writer can reuse, revive (un-use or un-revoke), extend or move a link to another user**, or use one that expired.
- **Writer grants:** `password_resets`: INSERT, UPDATE (also for `FOR UPDATE`), DELETE (the cleanup), in `WRITER_PRIVILEGES` through `PASSWORD_RESET_TABLE_PRIVILEGES`; the counters use D-068's grants on `sign_in_throttles`. A test runs every reset function as a role made by `grantWriterRole`, shows `checkWriterConnection` returns `[]` and `extra_privilege` for TRUNCATE, TRIGGER or REFERENCES on `password_resets`; T-032's per-grant test covers the table too.
- **What the writer can still do with direct SQL, precisely** (beside D-067, D-068 and D-069):
  - **insert a pending link with a token hash of its choosing** for any account that can use one, asked by nobody or in an active system admin's name, made now and lasting 5 to 60 minutes, after revoking the account's pending one; then use it like anyone holding the token. This is no more than it can already do by setting `password_hash` (D-068), and like that it leaves the second factor in place;
  - revoke any pending link (with any of the three reasons), and delete any link (which only stops it working; a deleted link's token works again only if the writer inserts its hash anew, the power above);
  - move `password_changed_at` forward to now (which kills the account's links), and rewrite or delete the reset counters (lifting or imposing a wait);
  - read every token hash (useless without the token).

  **It can't:** insert a link the person asked for (`requested_by` NULL) for an account with no second factor in use, insert a link already used or revoked, back- or forward-dated, longer than an hour, for a disabled account or one that can't sign in, or in a non-admin's name; have two pending links for one account; un-use, un-revoke or re-date a settled link; change a link's expiry, user, token or requester; use a link after it expired, or one made before the account's last password change or "end all"; move `password_changed_at` back or past now; TRUNCATE the table or turn its triggers off; or skip any of this in replica mode (all tested as the writer role).
- **The cleanup** (`runCleanup`, `npm run db:cleanup`) also deletes links whose end was more than a day ago (`deleteOldPasswordResets`), keeping a day's record of who asked; `deleteExpiredSignInThrottles` already removes old `reset:` counters.
- **For T-040 and T-045:** `deliver` must honour its `AbortSignal` (stop the SMTP exchange when it fires): a send left running after the limit is abandoned but still holds a socket and may still deliver a link that was already revoked. `onError` gets raw database errors, and pg's `where` field can carry the query's bound values, which here include **the address typed**, when `log_parameter_max_length_on_error` is above 0 (D-068's note): T-045's logging must drop or redact `where` and `detail`, or check that setting is 0 at startup. The link carries only the token (for example `/reset#<token>`, a fragment so it isn't sent in the request line or Referer); the form posts it with the new password; the page sends `Referrer-Policy: no-referrer` and `Cache-Control: no-store`. The reset route answers `RESET_REQUESTED` as it is, and must not log the address typed beside the outcome. The sign-in page's "Forgot your password?" goes here; nothing signs the person in.
- **Accounts with no second factor in use: self-service resets refused (review L7, decided).** A request from the sign-in page for an account with no active factor in `second_factors` (T-038, D-077: never set up, or reset by a system admin) is answered `RESET_REQUESTED` like any other, after the same counting, but sends no link: the background work does the same locking read and dummy UPDATE, and `onSettled` hears `needs_admin`. Otherwise a mailbox alone would give a new password and then the setting up of the attacker's own authenticator. The database refuses it too: `password_resets_check_new` refuses a link with `requested_by` NULL unless `password_reset_has_factor` (constraint `password_resets_needs_factor`). A system admin's `sendPasswordReset` still works for such an account (they decide). **A reset never lifts D-077's enrolment-token requirement**: it doesn't touch factors, recovery codes or enrolment tokens, so after a system admin's reset of two-step sign-in, a password reset (which only an admin can then send) still leads only to set-up with the emailed token (tested: `second_step_token_required` stays true and `startEnrolment` without the token is `enrolment_token_required`). An admin's reset of two-step sign-in ends every session through `endAllSessionsForUser`, which moves `sessions_valid_after`, so every reset link sent before it dies (tested). Kept even where D-077's enrolment rule would already stop the attack: defence in depth.
- **Left for later:** an audit log entry per request and use (with the sign-in event log); notifying the person by email after a reset (T-040, OWASP); a forced reset for every account when hash settings change (U-004, D-068's rule).
- Sources: OWASP ASVS 5.0 V6 (6.3.8, 6.4.1–6.4.3), V7 (7.4.2) and OWASP Forgot Password, Authentication and Session Management cheat sheets (github.com/OWASP/CheatSheetSeries), as read for D-052 and D-068 on 2026-11-23; PostgreSQL 16 docs `sql-createtrigger.html`, `sql-altertable.html` (`ENABLE ALWAYS TRIGGER`), `functions-datetime.html` (`statement_timestamp()`, `clock_timestamp()`), `explicit-locking.html` (row locks), `sql-update.html` (Read Committed re-checks the `WHERE` of a row another transaction changed); Node.js `crypto` docs (`randomBytes`, `createHash`).

## D-079 — The staff JSON API: a session wrapper over the service, CSRF in a header, the working site in a header, fixed codes with core's issues, a per-session limit, search over whole sites under a statement_timeout, and the D-076 gate for template and identifier type saves (2026-11-24, T-042)
Builds T-042 on D-051/D-058 (the HTTP layer), D-067 (the service is the only writer), D-069 (sessions), D-072/D-074 (roles and sites), D-073 (the admin's fetch wrapper and the dry-run check) and D-076. `packages/api/src/staff-api.ts` (`staffApiRoutes`), `rate-limit.ts`; `packages/service/src/definitions.ts` and `checkRecord`/`searchRecords` in `service.ts`; `packages/db/src/definitions.ts` and a `sites` option on `searchRecords`. No migration, no new dependency.
- **Endpoints (admin hostname only, D-034, D-052; never served to a public site):**
  - `GET /api/session`: the signed-in person, organization, limits and the CSRF token (`csrfTokenFor` of the cookie's token, D-069) for the admin to send back. Never the session token.
  - `GET /api/records/:id` (ETag `"v<version>"`), `POST /api/records` (`{ record, identifiers?, reason? }`, 201 with ETag and `Location`), `PUT /api/records/:id` (`If-Match: "v<n>"` required, `{ record, identifiers?, confirmDropped?, confirmTemplateMove?, reason? }`), `DELETE /api/records/:id` (`If-Match` required, optional `{ reason }`).
  - `POST /api/records/check` (`{ record, recordId? }`): the dry run D-073 asked for. `200 { issues }` with core's `Issue[]` from today's checks (validation, the template, links, record type, arrangement), nothing written; refused as the save would be when the person may not make or edit the record. A new parent, represented record or home site first goes through the save's own visibility and permission step (`intoPlace`/`moveIssues`), so one out of sight gets exactly the issue a missing one gets, byte for byte, and its level or kind is never checked (review B1, tested). The same holds for an unchanged parent out of sight (a system manager filed the unit across sites) when the unit's level changes: `recordCheckIssues`' `hiddenParent` answers `missing_parent` instead of a `wrong_level` naming the parent's level, in the dry run, saves and restores alike (second review). Identifier numbers and a published record's publish check are left to the save.
  - `GET /api/search?q=&limit=&after=`: staff search (D-066). `limit` at most 100 over HTTP (the result cap; 200 in the database function).
  - `GET /api/templates`, `GET /api/templates/:key[?version=]` (ETag `"v<n>"`), `PUT /api/templates/:key` (`If-None-Match: *` for a new key or `If-Match: "v<n>"`; `{ template, confirm?, confirmPublished? }`).
  - `GET /api/identifier-types` (each with its `tag`), `PUT /api/identifier-types/:key` (`If-None-Match: *` or `If-Match: "t-<tag>"`; `{ identifierType, confirm?, confirmPublished? }`).
- **Every request**, in this order: the server's own rules (path, body size 1 MiB and type `application/json` only, cookies, and for changes the `Origin`/`Sec-Fetch-Site` check against `OCULUS_ORIGINS`, D-058); the `__Host-oculus_session` cookie, else `401 unauthenticated`; on every method but GET/HEAD the `X-Oculus-CSRF` header checked with `checkCsrfToken` (HMAC-derived, `timingSafeEqual`) **before** the database is asked, else `403 csrf_failed`; `resolveSession` (full stage only: a session waiting for its second step, an expired or ended one and an unknown token are all the same `401`); the per-session limit; then the context from the session alone (`contextFromSession`: organization and `user:<id>`) plus the working site. **The working site** comes from the `X-Oculus-Site` header (a site ID, checked for form here; whether the person works there is the service's `unknown_site`, the same for another organization's, another site's and a made-up one). *Rejected:* the site in the path (`/api/sites/:site/records`): a record's own operations are checked at its home site with all the person's grants (D-074), so the path would be redundant everywhere but creation and could disagree with the record. `X-Oculus-CSRF` and `X-Oculus-Site` join D-058's request header allowlist.
- **Replies.** Data as JSON with `Cache-Control: no-store` (D-058). Refusals keep D-058's `{ "error": code }`; three kinds also carry core's `issues` (fixed codes; messages are core's own, which may quote the person's own input as D-067 says, never a database's text): `422 invalid` (a draft or option failing a check), `412 version_conflict` (with T-031's names-only `conflict`), `409 confirmation_required` (template or identifier type changes to confirm). Mapping: `unknown_record`/`invalid_id` on the record ID and `unknown_template` → `404 not_found` (nothing else in the body, so a missing record, another site's and another organization's are byte for byte the same, tested); `not_permitted` → 403; database faults (`internal_error`, `writer_role_refused`, unreadable stored data) → `500 internal_error`; `retry`, `timeout`, `search_timeout` → 503; `missing_reference`, `refused_by_database` → `409 conflict`; `If-Match` missing → `428 precondition_required`, malformed → `400 bad_if_match`.
- **Logs.** The service's `onError` and `resolveSession`'s go through `redactedError`: the error's class name and its SQLSTATE or Node system code, read from own data properties, and the operation's name. Never the message (a driver's message can quote a value), `detail` (a unique violation's key), `where` (bound parameters, D-068) or the stack. Records are fixed-shape (`ApiLogRecord`), to the caller's `log` or one JSON line on standard error.
- **Rate limit (cheap, per session).** An in-memory fixed one-minute window per session and kind: reads 600, writes 120, checks 600, searches 60 a minute; over it, `429 rate_limited`. A brake on a loop, not a defence against the unauthenticated, who are refused at one indexed lookup. Per process (D-035's one-process deployment); memory bounded at 50,000 windows. *Rejected:* a PostgreSQL counter (a write per request) and a Fastify plugin (its own dependency decision).
- **Staff search over HTTP (D-066's condition).** The service's `searchRecords` runs the query under `SET LOCAL statement_timeout` (`searchTimeoutMs`, default 5 s, at most 30 s); a search stopped by it is `503 search_timeout` (tested by holding a lock on `records` from another connection). Results are only the records of **whole sites** the person may see: a system manager every record; anyone else the sites of their whole-catalog grants holding `records.view` (all the organization's sites for a grant on every site; never records on no site). The filter is in the SQL (`sites` option of `searchRecords`, which only narrows), so neither a hit, a page's length nor its `next` says that another site's record matched. **Scoped grants (a unit's subtree) don't widen search yet**: filtering by subtree in SQL is a later task (T-043 or a follow-up), and filtering a page afterwards would leak hidden matches through short pages. Sharing (T-041b) must add accepted sites here.
- **Templates and identifier types (D-076's open item).** Reading needs `records.view` anywhere or `catalog.definitions`. Saving needs `catalog.definitions`, **which only system managers hold (John, 2026-11-24, D-072)**, and runs in one transaction: the arrangement lock first for anyone with scoped grants (the order record saves take locks in), then the definition (a template's `definition_lock` exclusively; an identifier type's row `FOR UPDATE`, which record saves, identifier saves and publishing wait for, since they take `FOR SHARE`); compare the version (`expectedVersion`, 0 for new) or tag (a SHA-256 of the definition with sorted keys; identifier types have no version column, so no migration was needed); run core's `templateChanges` over every record using the template (at most 50,000) or `identifierTypeChanges`; require `confirm` to list exactly the changes needing confirmation (`needs_confirmation`, path `["confirm", code, key]`); then **the published records whose public output the change may alter at once**:
  - *Templates:* when the template's public shape changes (the record kinds it applies to, a key added or removed, or anything in a field's definition but its `label` and `help`: visibility, inheritance, kind, repeatability, `required`, `maxLength`, list options with their labels, vocabularies, identifier types, record kinds, standards; `publicShapeChanged` compares each field's canonical JSON without those two properties), every published record using the template **or below one** (through `parent_id`/`represents_id`, `publishedAmong(…, { below: true })`). Core's change list alone was not enough (review B2, B3): a new public inherited key that no record holds is no change to it, yet it shows an unpublished parent's value on a published child, and removing a non-inherited "stopper" key from an intermediate template lets a grandparent's value through to a published leaf. Public output drops an inherited value that fails the child's own field definition, so a field that accepts more values (a `maxLength` raised or removed, a list option added) puts a value that was never public on a published child (second review B5), and an option's label is public text. Every published record whose output can change through this template is in the list, provided its output depends on this template only through its own template or its ancestors' (D-013 inheritance, `parent_id`/`represents_id`); records whose output changes for other reasons (a linked record's template) are not counted, and a change counted may leave a record's output as it was; a change of labels or help text alone asks nothing. All IDs come from the records read for the check (at most 50,000), not from core's lists capped at 10,000 (review item 1).
  - *Identifier types:* when the type becomes public (or a new public type adopts left-over numbers), every published record holding a number of it (holdings or a `{ type, value }` in `fields`) **or below such a record**, since an inherited identifier field carries the value down (review B4). For a new type, which has no row a publication could wait on, every record in that set is locked `FOR SHARE` before the states are read, so a publication in the meantime waits for COMMIT or has committed and is counted (review item 2); a record moved under a holder at the same moment is not covered (moves lock the parent only `FOR KEY SHARE`), an accepted gap for a rare system manager's action.
  - The person must hold `records.publish` where each of them is (always true for a system manager; kept as a second line and tested directly, since the service never reaches it with anyone else), else `403 not_permitted` with no count or ID; then `confirmPublished: true`, else `409` with `publishes_records` and one `published_record_affected` per record.
