Both break D-002: Electron ships Chromium's FFmpeg under the LGPL 2.1, and the `embedded-postgres` binaries for Windows ship `libintl` and `libiconv` (LGPL), while its Linux build carries OpenSSL 1.1, which is past end of life. **John chose to stay inside D-002** (2026-11-24): no Electron, no bundled PostgreSQL. Amends PLATFORM_PLAN decision 4 ("web only") to "web first".

- **`npm run desktop`** (`packages/api/src/desktop-cli.ts`; `scripts/start-oculus.cmd` on Windows, which installs, builds after each `git pull` and starts it). It refuses a database that isn't set up (pointing to `npm run setup`) or isn't Oculus's (`databaseBeforeSetup`, the check T-055 uses), applies new migrations, and starts the admin's server (`buildAdminServer`, D-081) on **127.0.0.1 only** as a development server (`NODE_ENV=development`: one person on one address, so D-068's client-address rule doesn't apply, and without the `OCULUS_SMTP_…` settings, D-085, reset and invitation messages are only logged). `OCULUS_ORIGINS` and `OCULUS_ADMIN_ORIGIN` are exactly `http://127.0.0.1:<port>`, so a DNS-rebinding page's writes fail the Origin check and its reads carry no session cookie. Port 4780, the settings' `port`, or a free one.
- **The window** is an installed Edge or Chrome (Edge first, as every Windows 11 has it; `OCULUS_DESKTOP_BROWSER` overrides; snap builds aren't on the list, as they can't write a hidden profile folder) in app mode, with Oculus's own profile folder (`%LOCALAPPDATA%\\Oculus\\window` on Windows, since browser caches don't belong in the roaming folder), no extensions, no background mode and no sync (so nothing, passwords included, goes to a browser account). Its own profile makes it its own browser process, so the launcher stops the server when it exits (on macOS, when it's quit). A window that exits at once was handed to a running browser, and the launcher then keeps running until Ctrl+C. No browser found, or it can't be started: the default browser and Ctrl+C. Ctrl+C, closing the console (SIGHUP) and Ctrl+Break all stop it (a second one, or 20 s of hanging, ends the process at once), and whatever fails after the server listens, the window, the server, the pool and the lock are all closed.
- **One launcher at a time.** `desktop.lock` holds the PID, a random token, the database's label (user, host or socket folder, port and name, never a password) and, once listening, the address. It is created whole (written to a private temporary file, then hard-linked into place, which fails if a lock exists) and rewritten by rename, so no start reads half a lock. A second start trusts a lock with an address only if that address answers `/desktop/instance?challenge=<fresh random>` (a route the launcher adds through `buildAdminServer`'s new `extraRoutes`, answering only for its own `127.0.0.1:<port>` Host) with an HMAC of the challenge under the token, so a reused PID after a reboot or another program on that port never passes, and a captured answer can't be replayed. Then it opens another window on the running server, or refuses if that one serves another database. No answer in 5 s while the PID lives is "busy", never stale. Before the address is written, a live PID and a lock under ten minutes old mean "already starting". Anything else is stale: renamed aside (only one start can move it), checked to be the stale lock (else linked back) and replaced. A start that finds its lock lost when it writes its address stops.
- **The settings folder:** `OCULUS_DESKTOP_HOME`, else `%APPDATA%\Oculus`, `~/Library/Application Support/Oculus` or `$XDG_CONFIG_HOME/oculus`, made 0700. `desktop.json` holds `databaseUrl` and `port` only; unknown settings are refused, and so is **a password in the address** (it goes in the pgpass file, as everywhere in Oculus).
- **The secret key** (D-077) is made once from 32 random bytes into `secret-key` with `O_EXCL` and mode 0600 (a key file others can read is refused on POSIX, as ssh does; on Windows the per-user AppData folder's permissions). `OCULUS_SECRET_KEY` wins. **It is never made for a database that already holds two-step set-ups** (`second_factors` has rows): a missing key there is a lost key, and a new one would lock everyone out, so the launcher refuses and asks for the backup. It also refuses a key (file or `OCULUS_SECRET_KEY`) whose fingerprint, with any `OCULUS_SECRET_KEY_PREVIOUS`, doesn't cover every fingerprint the sealed secrets carry (bytes 2–9 of `secret_box`). A key file left empty by a crash is made again while nothing is sealed. It's never printed, and neither is the database password; the database is named by a password-free label (`oculus on localhost`). DESKTOP.md tells the person to back it up, since database backups don't hold it.
- **For now the server connects as the database's owner**, like the e2e server (superseded by D-093). T-057 makes the owner and writer roles (D-067) and the database on the person's PostgreSQL, saves the passwords in pgpass, and writes `desktop.json`.

**Rejected:** Electron and `embedded-postgres` (D-002, above); a D-002 exception for an optional desktop package (John chose against it); Tauri or another WebView wrapper (the platform WebView is fine license-wise, but it adds a Rust toolchain to build and a second UI shell to keep secure, for little more than an app window gives); opening a normal browser tab (no window of its own, and no way to tell when it closes); listening on every address (the admin would be on the local network with no TLS).

**Sources:** Chromium command-line switches `--app`, `--user-data-dir`, `--no-first-run` (https://peter.sh/experiments/chromium-command-line-switches/); Electron 45's `LICENSES.chromium.html` (FFmpeg, LGPL 2.1); the `@embedded-postgres/windows-x64` 18.7.0-beta.19 and `linux-x64` package contents; OpenSSL 1.1.1 end of life (https://openssl-library.org/post/2023-09-11-eol-111/); OWASP ASVS 5.0 V13 (secrets kept out of code and logs).

## D-089 — Serving stored files: a handler sends bytes only through `fileReply`, which never sends an active stored type, shows only plain images, audio and video, downloads everything else as `application/octet-stream`, and gives every file reply nosniff, a sandboxing CSP and same-origin CORP (2026-11-24, M-001)

Builds M-001 on D-058 (and its S-025 amendment), D-034 and D-051. `packages/api/src/file-replies.ts`, wired into `server.ts` (rule 11). M-003 plugs its download and preview routes into it; M-002 stores the sniffed type it relies on.

- **One way to send a file.** A route handler returns `fileReply({ body, size, mediaType, fileName, disposition })`; a look-alike object is sent as JSON, never as a file (a `WeakSet` of replies `fileReply` made). The server then drops every header the handler set (cookies included), sends exactly the reply's Content-Type, Content-Disposition and Content-Length with a 200, and puts `FILE_SECURITY_HEADERS` in place of the API's in onSend. `type()` and `header()` keep D-058's rules for everything else, so a handler still can't send HTML or XML.
- **Never the stored type as it is.** Only `INLINE_FILE_TYPES` (JPEG, PNG, GIF, WebP and AVIF images; MPEG, Ogg, WAV, WebM, FLAC and MP4 audio; MP4, WebM and Ogg video) may be shown, and only when the route asks for `inline`; they are sent as the bare essence, without parameters. Everything else (HTML, SVG, XHTML and every XML type, scripts, CSS, PDF, plain text, TIFF, unknown and malformed types) is `application/octet-stream` with `Content-Disposition: attachment`. Rejected: sending passive non-media types such as PDF or text inline (a PDF viewer is script-capable, text may be read as another type by an old client, and the sandbox blocks viewers anyway); serving downloads under their stored type (one table to get wrong).
- **Every file reply** has `X-Content-Type-Options: nosniff`; `Content-Security-Policy: default-src 'none'; sandbox; frame-ancestors 'none'; form-action 'none'; base-uri 'none'` (a document opened from it has an opaque origin and runs nothing, and no other page may frame it); `Cross-Origin-Resource-Policy: same-origin` (another site, a public site included, can't embed it); `Referrer-Policy: no-referrer`; `Cache-Control: private, no-store`. The admin shows previews with `<img>` on its own origin, where its own CSP applies.
- **File names** go only into Content-Disposition (RFC 6266 §4.3): an ASCII fallback (`filename="…"`) and RFC 8187 `filename*=UTF-8''…`. Control, format (bidirectional, zero-width), private-use and surrogate characters are taken out, path separators, quotes and Windows-reserved characters become `_`, leading and trailing dots and spaces go, at most 150 characters, `file` if nothing is left. A name never reaches a path.
- **Exact length and streams.** `size` must equal the bytes given. A stream is given as a function that opens it (`body: () => Readable`), called only when the reply goes out, so a reply that is never sent (the handler threw after making it, the request ended first) opens nothing. The server hijacks the reply and sends it outside Fastify's handler timeout (which otherwise cuts any download at 30 seconds): it waits for the first bytes before writing the headers, so a file that can't be read is the ordinary `500 {"error":"internal_error"}` with the API's headers, then `pipeline(source, ExactLength, response)`, so a storage error, a stream longer or shorter than its size, or a client that goes away destroys the source and the response together (never more than Content-Length sent). Every download ends within `SERVER_TIMEOUTS.download` (2 hours), and closing the server stops downloads in flight. A client going away isn't logged; the other ends are logged as fixed codes (`file_stream_storage`, `_length`, `_deadline`, `_closing`). A chunk that isn't bytes (an object-mode stream, a string from `setEncoding`) cuts the download off as a wrong length instead of throwing. A stream that is already closed, failed or at its end (with a non-zero size) is the 500 at once. Once the server starts closing, a download that would start is `503` with `Connection: close`. HEAD sends the headers and opens nothing. A Buffer is sent the ordinary way. Limits on concurrent downloads and slow readers per client are S-035's.
- **Left to others.** Serving files from an origin of their own apart from the public sites, and which host answers `/api` and files, is T-050's. Byte ranges (video seeking), ETags and caching are M-003's or later. Thumbnails are images Oculus makes, served the same way.
- **Tests.** `file-replies.attacks.test.ts`: over inject, 20 active, odd and malformed stored types × each disposition are downloads as octet-stream; handler-set types, ETags, status and cookies don't survive; errors from file routes keep the API's headers; forged replies; header-breaking, traversal, bidirectional, device and over-long names. Over real HTTP with a counting source: a storage error with work between `fileReply` and return (no uncaught exception), a file that can't be read (a 500, the connection kept), an opener that throws, a handler that throws after making a reply (nothing opened), a slow file sent whole past a 300 ms handler timeout, the download deadline, the server closing mid-download, a client that hangs up, streams long and short, HEAD (nothing opened) and a reply returned after the timeout (nothing opened). `packages/admin/e2e/files.spec.ts` (Chromium): a page on another origin frames, sandbox-frames, `<object>`s, `<embed>`s and `<img>`s HTML, SVG, XHTML and an SVG stored as PNG, and no script runs (a control frame proves the listener works, every file is requested; with the file rules broken the same test sees 40 runs). Framing is stopped by several headers at once (frame-ancestors, CORP, the sandbox, the type), so this test doesn't single out one of them; the unit tests check each. Opening HTML, SVG or XHTML directly downloads it; an SVG stored as PNG is not rendered as SVG.
- Sources: WHATWG MIME Sniffing §4.6 and §7; RFC 9110 §8.3; RFC 6266 and RFC 8187; MDN on Content-Security-Policy `sandbox`, `X-Content-Type-Options` and `Cross-Origin-Resource-Policy`; OWASP ASVS 5.0 V5.3 (file download) and the OWASP File Upload Cheat Sheet.

## D-090 — Hosts: the staff API on the admin's host only, the admin's origin required in production, public hosts mapped to sites, and the public sites' connection checked as a public reader at startup (2026-11-24, T-050a)
Builds T-050a on D-034 (one app), D-052 (the admin on its own hostname, ASVS 3.5.4), D-058 (the server's rules), D-080 (the public reader role) and D-081 (the admin server). `packages/api/src/server.ts` (rule 12), `public-sites.ts` (new), `admin-server.ts`, `admin-cli.ts`. No migration, no new dependency. T-050 is split: T-050b serves public record reads (it needs stored Dublin Core mappings first), T-050c OAI-PMH.
- **`/api` answers on the admin's host only.** When the server has an admin origin, every request whose path is `/api` or under it, on any host but the admin's (scheme and host as the request arrives, `X-Forwarded-*` only from a trusted proxy), is answered `404 not_found` in the first `onRequest` hook: the reply an unknown path gets, byte for byte, before any route, cookie or session is looked at. So a public site's pages can't reach the staff or sign-in routes through their own host, and nothing there asks for the session cookie (host-only `__Host-`, D-052). `/health` stays on every host. Without an admin origin (tests, development) nothing changes. *Rejected:* refusing unlisted hosts altogether now (the desktop launcher and development reach the server by more than one name; the Origin check already guards every change); a separate Fastify instance per host (D-034's one app, and D-058's rules would have to be kept twice).
- **In production the admin's origin is required** (`OCULUS_ADMIN_ORIGIN`), so the rule above always holds where it matters; `buildAdminServer` refuses to start without it, naming the variable.
- **`OCULUS_PUBLIC_SITES`** maps public origins to sites: `<origin>=<site id>,…`. Each origin must be in `OCULUS_ORIGINS`, may be listed once and may not be the admin's; two origins may show the same site (`www.` and bare). Each site must exist; its organization is read once at startup with the writer's pool, since the public reader can read no table and `readPublicRecord` takes both.
- **The public sites' connection** (`OCULUS_PUBLIC_DATABASE_URL`, `publicDb`) is required when public sites are listed and, whenever given, must pass `checkPublicReaderConnection` (D-080): a connection that can read any table (the writer's, the owner's, a superuser's) is refused at startup, the error naming only the problems' codes, never the URL. "After every migration" (D-080) is met by the restart a migration is followed by; T-051's runbook says so. *Rejected:* opening the public pool from the writer's URL with another role name (two credentials must be two settings; a missing one must stop the start, not be guessed).
- **Review fixes (2026-11-24).** "Not the admin's" compares hostnames, not origins (cookies aren't kept apart by port or scheme, so `https://admin.example.org:8443` as a public site would receive the admin's session cookie). A site ID held by two organizations (D-060 allows it) is refused rather than mapped to whichever row comes first. The reader must reach the writer's own database (same database OID, name and server start time), so a reader aimed at a copy or staging install is refused. Shutting down closes both pools whatever happens to either.
- **Tests:** `/api` on the admin's host (directly and as https behind a trusted proxy) and on public, `www.` and unlisted hosts, GET and POST, known and unknown API paths (identical 404 bodies, no cookie), `/health` everywhere, and no change without an admin origin; the public-sites list's refusals; the start refused in production without the admin's origin, with public sites but no reader, with a reader that can read tables (the owner), with a reader of another database, with a site ID two organizations hold and with a site that doesn't exist; and a start with a real public reader role.
- Sources: OWASP ASVS 5.0 V3.5.4 (separate hostnames for applications with different trust), V13 (configuration); RFC 9110 §7.2 (`Host`); PostgreSQL 16 `ddl-priv.html`, as cited in D-080.

## D-092 — Updates from an uploaded package: a signed ZIP checked before it is parsed, unpacked beside the running release, migrations and a backup before one atomic switch of `current`, rollback only while the database is unchanged (2026-11-24, T-060, John: "upload a zip to upgrade the program on the server")

Builds PLATFORM_PLAN §9 ("a signed update package, verified with Node's built-in Ed25519 signing, with a safely kept private key and a backup key") on D-035, D-064, D-072, D-079 and D-084. `packages/api/src/update-zip.ts`, `update-package.ts`, `update-manager.ts`, `update-api.ts`, `release-cli.ts` (`npm run release`); `authorizeSystemUpdate` in `packages/service/src/people.ts`; the admin's `src/settings/Updates.tsx` (a tab of System settings). `packages/db/src/grant-cli.ts`. No new dependency (`node:crypto`, `node:zlib` and `node:fs` only), no migration.

- **The risk.** Installing an update runs new code on the server with the server's own rights and secrets. Whoever can make the server accept a package owns the server. So acceptance rests on a signature the server can check, not on who uploaded it: a stolen system manager session alone can't install anything.
- **The package.** A ZIP made only by `npm run release -- pack`: the built packages (`dist`), the migrations, the built admin, the production dependencies (found the way Node resolves them from the server's four packages, `@oculus/*` as real copies, never links; dev dependencies and sources left out), `oculus-release.json` (the version) and two control files: `oculus-update.json` (format 1, product, `MAJOR.MINOR.PATCH` version, `builtAt`, notes, the migrations, every file's path, size and SHA-256) and `oculus-update.sig` (`{ keyId, signature }`, Ed25519 over the manifest's exact bytes). The release command also signs; the key is a PKCS#8 PEM made by `npm run release -- keygen`, readable by its owner only, and never on the server or in the repository.
- **Trusted keys.** `OCULUS_UPDATE_KEYS` lists one to eight `ed25519:<base64url of 32 bytes>` keys: the main key and a backup key kept elsewhere. A key ID is the first 16 hex digits of the SHA-256 of the raw key. Replacing a lost key is changing this variable on the server.
- **Checking, in order, refusing at the first problem.** The ZIP's shape (below); the signature by a listed key, **before the manifest is parsed**; the manifest's exact shape; exactly the listed files in the archive, no more and no fewer; each file's size, CRC-32 and SHA-256. *Rejected:* trusting the uploader (a session is not a signature); a checksum without a signature (anyone can make one); signing the ZIP's bytes as a whole (the manifest's signature covers every file through its hash, and lets the server refuse before reading any file).
- **The ZIP reader accepts only what the writer makes.** No local extra fields, no archive comment or appended bytes, no bytes before or between entries, no overlapping entries, no ZIP64, one disk; regular files only (no links, folders or other Unix types), no encryption or data descriptors, stored or deflate only, names UTF-8-flagged printable ASCII with no `..`, `.`, empty segment, backslash, colon, leading `/` or trailing dot or space; names that differ only in case, or a file that is another's folder, refused (case-insensitive disks). Limits: 256 MiB per package (`maxPackageBytes`), 60,000 entries (the writer refuses more too), 64 MiB per file, 768 MiB unpacked; inflating stops at the declared size. *Rejected:* a ZIP library (a dependency to vet for a format we fully control; general readers accept what we refuse); tar (no random access, and Windows has no built-in tool for the publisher).
- **Upload in parts, through the HTTP layer as it is.** Parts of 512 KiB raw bytes, each strict canonical base64 in an ordinary JSON body, in order (`PUT …/parts/:index`), the size given at the start. D-051's parsers, the 1 MiB limit, the 30-second timeouts and the per-session rate limit all stay unchanged. *Rejected:* a new `application/zip` parser with a larger limit and a longer timeout (weakens rules every route relies on, and one slow upload would need a timeout far above 30 s).
- **Who may.** System managers only (`system.settings`), checked by the service in every request. Installing and rolling back also need the password and a code entered in the last 10 minutes (D-084's rule, `403 reauthentication_required`). Uploading and checking don't: nothing runs until an install.
- **The folder (`OCULUS_UPDATE_DIR`).** `releases/<version>/`, `current` (a link to the running release), `uploads/`, `backups/`, `state.json` (the waiting version and the last 50 installs and rollbacks, each with the person's user ID), `update.lock`. The host's start command is `node <folder>/current/packages/api/dist/admin-cli.js` (`admin-cli` and `release-cli` compare their real paths, so they start through the link). The first install on a server is `npm run release -- install <package> --dir <folder>` from any unpacked copy; later ones go through the admin.
- **Installing.** The package is unpacked into a new folder (each file created exclusively) and renamed into `releases/<version>` only once every file matched; only a version newer than the running one is accepted; the server must be running from `current` (else a restart wouldn't start the new code). If the release brings migrations the running one lacks, three steps run as child processes **connected as the tables' owner** (`OCULUS_UPDATE_MIGRATE_DATABASE_URL`, given only to these children; the app keeps its writer URL, D-063): a database backup with the running release's `db:backup` into `backups/` (D-064; the newest 5 kept; `OCULUS_UPDATE_BACKUP=skip` turns it off), the new release's `db:migrate`, then the new `grant-cli.js` giving `OCULUS_UPDATE_WRITER_ROLE` the writer's privileges again, so tables a migration made are usable. The scripts are started by their real paths (they compare their own path with the one they were started by). Without the owner URL and role, a release with migrations is refused from the admin (`migrations_need_owner`) and installed with `npm run release -- install` over the host's shell instead. *John decided (2026-11-25):* the Historical Society's server gets the owner URL and writer role, so every update installs from the admin, as WordPress, Omeka S and Nextcloud do; installing still needs a system manager's fresh password and code and a package signed with John's key. The cost: whoever takes over the running app also gets the owner's password, and with it the power to switch off the append-only history and the other database protections. Oculus still supports leaving it unset (database-changing updates then go through `npm run release -- install`), for organizations with someone to run that; the setup guide offers both. Only after these steps is `current` replaced in one `rename` of a new link. A failure before then leaves the running release current and the package waiting, but a failed migration may have applied some of its files (each runs in its own transaction): the message says so and names the backup to restore. Each failed step's output is kept in `logs/` in the updates folder (owner-only), never in a reply. Between migrating and the restart, the old code runs on the new schema: with `OCULUS_UPDATE_RESTART=none` the screen asks for the restart at once. Three releases are kept besides the current, the waiting and the rollback one; half-unpacked folders left by a stopped server are removed at the next check.
- **Keep the updates folder out of the web.** It holds database backups: on SiteGround it must not be inside `public_html` (T-051).
- **Restarting.** `OCULUS_UPDATE_RESTART=none` (default): the screen asks a person to restart Oculus from the host's panel. `exit`: the server closes cleanly and exits 0 for the host's process manager to start the new version. Which one SiteGround's Node.js manager needs is left to T-051. *Rejected:* restarting itself in place (no portable way across hosts, D-035).
- **Rolling back** points `current` at the release that ran before the last install, only when both carry the same migrations. When the database was migrated it is refused (`database_changed`): the older code can't be trusted on a newer schema, and the way back is restoring the backup taken before the install (D-064).
- **One at a time.** One job (check, install, rollback) at a time in the server, one part written at a time, and a lock file shared with the release command: written whole under a temporary name and hard-linked into place, removed only by its holder, and taken over only when its process is gone.
- **Pace.** Each part counts against the per-session limit on changes (120 a minute); the screen waits and sends a part again when told to slow down, so a large package goes up at about 60 MB a minute.
- **Logs.** `update_event` records with the step and a version only; never a path, key or output of the child scripts. The child scripts get the server's environment (they need `OCULUS_DATABASE_URL`), and their output is not shown in the screen.
- **Known cost.** Checking runs inflate and SHA-256 on the server's main thread, so other requests wait during a check of a large package (a few seconds for this build's 6.5 MB).
- **Where it lives.** A fourth tab, Updates, in T-048a's System settings (D-087), system managers only.
- **Left for later (roadmap):** installs and rollbacks in the people change log (`people_events` has no kind for them; a migration, T-060a).
- Sources: Node.js 22 `crypto.sign`/`crypto.verify` (Ed25519 with `null` algorithm), `crypto.createPublicKey` (JWK `OKP`/`Ed25519`), `zlib.crc32`, `zlib.inflateRawSync` (`maxOutputLength`), `fs.rename` (atomic replace on POSIX); PKWARE APPNOTE.TXT 6.3.10 (§4.3.7 local header, §4.3.12 central directory, §4.3.16 end of central directory, §4.4.4 general purpose bits 0, 3 and 11, §4.4.15 external attributes); RFC 8032 (Ed25519); OWASP ASVS 5.0 V15.2 (dependency and component integrity), V12 (file handling: path traversal, archive extraction, zip bombs); CWE-22, CWE-59, CWE-409; the Zip Slip advisory (Snyk, 2018).

## D-093 — The desktop database: an owner and a writer role made with the postgres password typed once, SCRAM verifiers instead of passwords on the wire, the passwords only in pgpass, and the launcher serving as the writer (2026-11-24, Chat D, T-057)
Builds T-057 on D-088 (the launcher), D-067 (one writer, `grantWriterRole`, `checkWriterConnection`) and T-055 (setup).

- **`npm run desktop:database`** (`packages/api/src/desktop-database-cli.ts`; on Windows `start-oculus.cmd` runs it by itself when there's no `desktop.json`). It asks for the `postgres` account's password once (kept in memory, never printed or saved), connects to `OCULUS_DESKTOP_ADMIN_URL` (default `postgresql://postgres@localhost:5432/postgres`), and refuses before changing anything if `desktop.json` already names a database, the server is older than 15, or the database `oculus` or the roles `oculus_owner` and `oculus_app` exist.
- **Two plain LOGIN roles**, no attributes and no memberships: the owner owns the database (made by `createDatabase`, then `ALTER DATABASE … OWNER`) and runs migrations; the writer gets `grantWriterRole` only. Names go through the server's `format('%I')`; passwords through `%L` as **SCRAM-SHA-256 verifiers** computed here (RFC 5802/7677, PostgreSQL's stored form), so a plain password never reaches the server or a statement log.
- **Passwords:** 32 random bytes each, written only to the pgpass file (`PGPASSFILE`, else `%APPDATA%\\postgresql\\pgpass.conf` or `~/.pgpass`), new lines first, `:` and `\\` escaped, mode 0600. `desktop.json` gets `databaseUrl` (the writer) and `ownerDatabaseUrl` (the owner), never a password. Then setup's own questions run (T-055) as the owner.
- **Undo and resume:** if anything fails after the first role is made, it drops the database and both roles and takes its own lines back out of pgpass (existing bytes kept exactly), so the person can run it again. `desktop.json` is written last (merged with any `port` already there, by rename); once it names the database, a later run doesn't start over but finishes the setup questions if they weren't (a mistyped second password, a closed window), and does nothing if they were. The launcher answers exit code 3 for that unfinished state (only when the owner address is `desktop.json`'s own), and `start-oculus.cmd` then runs the helper again, once. A run killed between making the roles and writing `desktop.json` (the console closed during migrations) is not undone; that person removes them in pgAdmin (S-034).
- **The launcher with both addresses** checks and migrates as the owner, refuses if the two reach different databases (`current_database()`, the server's start time and port), runs `checkWriterConnection` before granting, counting everything but `missing_privilege` (so a hand-edited `desktop.json` naming another account never gets the writer's grants, while a writer missing what a new migration's table needs is granted it), re-runs `grantWriterRole` after migrating (new tables need grants) and checks again, refuses to serve if it finds anything, and serves as the writer. `OCULUS_DATABASE_URL` given by hand still means one address for everything, as in D-088.

**Rejected:** a password in `desktop.json` or the environment (pgpass is the one place, as everywhere in Oculus); `CREATE ROLE … PASSWORD '<plain>'` (it can land in the server's log); serving as the owner (D-067: the owner can change the schema and switch off the history guards); asking the person to choose the roles' passwords (they'd never type them, and random ones are stronger).

**Sources:** RFC 5802 §3 and RFC 7677 (SCRAM-SHA-256); PostgreSQL 18 documentation, §20.5 "Password Authentication" (stored verifier format) and §32.16 "The Password File" (format, escaping, permissions).

## D-091 — The publish check runs the public output's template check, so a number that no longer fits a changed format fails it; the format change lists the published records it takes off public sites (2026-11-24, S-026)
Builds S-026 on D-016 (public output fails closed), D-031 (stranded values are read leniently, and type changes are confirmed first), D-038 (identifier fields show current identifiers) and D-054 (the publish check reads what it needs itself).
- **The choice: report it, don't show it.** Public output keeps refusing a record whose public identifier field shows a number that doesn't fit its type's format now (`format_mismatch`, shown as not available). The publish check (`publishCheckIssues`) now reports the same thing, so the record can't be published, and a published one shows the reason to staff instead of quietly dropping off the public sites.
- **Rejected: letting public output show the stored value.** It would keep such records on the public sites, but loosen a check that fails closed on purpose (D-016), and a format is often changed to correct numbers that were wrong. Public output and the publish check would also still disagree on everything else the template check refuses.
- **How.** Core's new `publicTemplateIssues(effective, checkers)` is the same template check `publicOutput` runs on the public values (`templateCheck` over `fullValues`), returning issues instead of throwing. `publishCheckIssues` adds its issues after `effectivePublishIssues`' own, with the snapshot's checkers, so anything `readPublicRecord` would refuse in that check also fails the publish check. `format_mismatch` gets a fixed message that says what happened and what to do. Like every publish check message, it quotes no number, type key or format. A stranded number in the record's identifiers that no public field shows doesn't hold the record back, because nothing public shows it.
- **What staff see.** Saving a record that still shows a misfit number is refused by the save's own template check (`format_mismatch`). Giving it a number that fits, and keeping the old one as a former identifier, is saved, and the record is back on the public sites without being published again.
- **Wider than identifiers, on purpose.** The publish check now applies every refusal of that template check (an unknown identifier type, a date, list or length check), not only `format_mismatch`. A published record that already breaks one of them was already not available on the public sites, and the publish check now says so. Since a published record's every write runs the publish check, its edits, identifier saves and restores are refused (`published_record_check`) until it is fixed or withdrawn. Withdrawing doesn't run the check, so such a record can always be taken down.
- **The format change's confirmation.** `identifierTypeChanges` takes an optional `existing.published` (the IDs of the published records). When it is given, `format_changed` also lists `publishedRecordIds` and `publishedRecordCount`, and its message gives the count, so a manager knows before confirming which published records public sites would show as not available. Those are the published records holding a misfit in a field public in their template (any field, without `templates`). Records that only inherit such a number aren't listed: `effectiveRecord` leaves out an inherited value its checkers refuse (D-013), so they stay on the public sites without it.
- **Left for later (S-033).** No service or screen edits identifier types yet, and `updateIdentifierType` takes a format change with no confirmation. Until S-033 is built, a format change still takes such records off the public sites without warning. Staff see why only in the publish check.
- Sources: none outside the project (an internal consistency rule).
- **Amendment (2026-11-25, Chat C, S-033): the service's identifier-type save lists the published records a new format takes off the public sites, and must be told exactly those.** `saveIdentifierType` (update) still asks its confirmations from `identifierTypeChanges` without `existing` (the third review's reason stands), and the format change keeps core's own message (which says records weren't checked) with one sentence added counting the published records it drops. When the change includes `format_changed`, it reads the published records holding the type (`publishedHoldingType`, under the type's exclusive lock, which publishing waits on), those records and the organization's templates, and calls core again with that `existing` (`published` included, no identifier sets: the list comes from the records' public `{ type, value }` fields) only to get `format_changed`'s `publishedRecordIds`. Those records are listed (`published_record_dropped` at `["droppedFromPublic", <id>]`) with whichever confirmation is asked first, and **the save needs `confirmDropped` naming exactly them** (`drops_published_records`, 409, otherwise): a confirmation sent before the list was seen, or one made stale by a record published meanwhile, asks again. The saved reply carries `droppedFromPublic`. A list that can't be made (core refuses the `existing`, or past `MAX_AFFECTED_PUBLISHED`, for which there is no smaller step yet) refuses the save. The list errs toward too many (records already unavailable, missing templates read as all public). A template save running at the same moment isn't locked against (templates aren't read under their lock here); public output still fails closed. New types have no published holders; there is no delete operation yet (one would call `identifierTypeDeletion` the same way). `readRecordsForChecks` is exported from `@oculus/db` for this.

## D-094 — Sharing items between sites: a share row per offer of a published item, decided by the second site's manager, and a read-only view for the second site that is exactly what the public sees, never through the record's own permission checks (2026-11-24, T-041b, T-041c)

Builds D-074's sharing on D-019, D-072, D-067 and D-076. `packages/db/migrations/0024_record_shares.sql`, `packages/db/src/shares.ts`, `publishedRecordView` in `packages/db/src/workflow.ts`, `packages/service/src/shares.ts`. T-041b is split: T-041c (this: storage and the service), T-041d (HTTP routes), T-041e (admin screens), T-041f (public sites showing accepted items, with T-050b).

- **One row per offer** (`record_shares`): the record (its own organization), the second site (any organization's, a composite key to `sites`), who offered it and when, and its state: `offered` → `accepted` | `declined` | `withdrawn`; `accepted` → `withdrawn` (home side) | `removed` (second site). The last three are final; offering again is a new row. At most one live share per record and site (a partial unique index; two offers at once get one share and `already_shared`, by `ON CONFLICT DO NOTHING`). The database refuses an offer to the record's own home site (read under the record's `FOR SHARE` lock, which a move waits for), any change to who, what or where, any other move, a decision set by withdrawing or removing, and times that aren't the moment of the change; its trigger is `ENABLE ALWAYS` (tested in replica mode; foreign keys and the cascade are PostgreSQL's own and, as for every table, don't fire in replica mode). Rows go with their record (`ON DELETE CASCADE`, run as the table's owner); the writer can't delete them. *Rejected:* a column of site IDs on the record (no history of who decided, and every record write would touch it); a state per (record, site) mixed into D-076's workflow state.
- **Only what the public can see, and the view is the public's.** An item can be offered only while it is published on a home site, and a second site sees it only while the public could see it at all: published, on a home site (D-080's rule), and passing the publish check public output runs (`effectivePublishIssues`: identifiers, rights, misfit values and, once stored, deeds and access restrictions). `SharedItemView` is then the title, kind and public values of `publicView(effectiveRecord(...))` (`publishedRecordView`: inherited public values in; staff-only fields, `record_link` values and their labels, rights notes, place record IDs and non-public identifiers out), with the names of its home site and organization, built after the operation's checks in one REPEATABLE READ, read-only snapshot of its own (`sharedItemViews`), which re-reads each share's state, under the service's error handling (retries, `onError`, clean refusals). An organization that keeps a template off its public pages by giving it no Dublin Core mapping still has its items shown on a second site (mappings aren't stored yet, T-050b): S-036. Which public fields a site's Dublin Core mapping puts on a page is the public page's business (T-041f with T-050b). Never history, rights settings or people (D-019). So sharing shows another site nothing the public couldn't already see, and the publish permission (D-036) still decides what goes out. Whether a second site of the same organization should see more is a question for John (asked 2026-11-24).
- **Who.** Offering and withdrawing: whoever may edit the record at its home site (`onRecord` with the edit permissions), who also sees the record's shares. Locks follow D-065: the arrangement lock (scoped grants only) before the record's row (`FOR SHARE`), and a withdrawal takes the record before the share, as a deletion does. Accepting, declining, removing and listing a site's offers: the second site's manager (`site.settings` there) or a system manager, in a session of the second site's organization. Seeing accepted items: anyone with `records.view` for the whole of that site's catalog; a unit-scoped grant doesn't cover them. The second site is named by its organization and site IDs, which its manager passes on: Oculus lists no other organization's sites. A share is read or locked only with its organization in the query (`lockShare`/`readShare` by side), so no other organization's session can touch or time it.
- **The record's own checks don't read shares.** `sitesSharedInto` (D-072's hook) stays empty, so `onRecord`, `allowsUnlocked` and every ordinary operation (read, edit, history, restore, offering it on) answer a shared item exactly as before for the second site's people, of either organization: as missing. The S-028 equivalence is untouched. *Rejected:* filling `sharedInto` for same-organization shares (it would show every staff field through `records.view`); a cross-organization `Access` (sessions work in exactly one organization, D-069).
- **Moves.** A record moved onto a site it was shared into is no longer listed or shown there as shared (it is the site's own item now); the share stays in the table, ended by nothing. Lists of offers and of accepted items are read separately (at most 200 each), and shares whose item the site couldn't be shown (not published, on no site, moved onto it) are left out in the query itself, before the limit, so offers can't push accepted items out. Shares whose item fails public output's publish check are left out only after the limit, so they can still take places in a page, and they show again when the item passes it again: S-036, with a cap on the cost of building views. A share hidden by a move stays live (it still blocks a new offer, and shows again if the item moves back): S-036.
- **Refusals that say nothing.** Another organization's share, one the person may not act on or see, and an unknown or malformed ID all answer `unknown_share`, byte for byte (tested for accept, decline, remove, withdraw and read); a record the person can't see is `unknown_record`; an unknown site, a site under the wrong organization and the record's own home site are the same `unknown_site`. Also `share_not_open`, `already_shared`, `not_published`. Accepted signals: `already_shared` and `not_published` to someone who may edit the record (they see both anyway), and `unknown_site` telling an editor whether a site ID they were given exists.
- **Left as S-036:** paging the lists past 200; a limit on offers from one organization to a site, and on re-offering straight after a decline; keeping the second organization's record of its decision when the home side deletes the item (the cascade removes it); ending a share when its item is moved onto the second site.
- **Tests** (`packages/service/src/shares.test.ts`): offering by role, by a unit-scoped person (inside and outside the scope, racing an edit of the unit), only when published, hidden and other-organization records, home, unknown and crossed sites, duplicates (including three offers at once) and bad requests; deciding only by the second site's manager, refusals byte for byte; accept and decline racing, withdrawal racing acceptance (ends withdrawn); the view (no staff value, link label or rights note anywhere in any list), a home edit showing, withdrawal from publication hiding it, a published item on no site never offered, public output refused after a template change hiding it, hidden shares left out before the limit; the second site's people of both organizations unable to read, edit, list versions of, offer on or withdraw the record; a move onto the second site; deletion; the database's own refusals, replica mode and the writer's lack of DELETE.

## D-096 — Stored Dublin Core mappings: one current mapping per template beside it, saved by system managers with D-076's confirmation, and read by the public reader through a second function (2026-11-24, T-050b)
Builds T-050b on D-012 (a mapping is data beside its template, checked against it at every read), D-016 (public output), D-047 (organizations), D-072 (definitions are system managers'), D-076 (published records shown a change at once), D-079 (the staff API) and D-080 (the public reader). Migration `0022_dublin_core_mappings.sql`, `packages/db/src/dublin-core-mappings.ts`, `workflow.ts` (`readPublicRecord`), `roles.ts`; `readDublinCoreMapping` and `saveDublinCoreMapping` in `packages/service/src/definitions.ts`; two routes in `packages/api/src/staff-api.ts`. No new dependency. T-050 is split further: T-050d serves public reads with these mappings.
- **The table.** `dublin_core_mappings (organization_id, template_key, version, mapping, changed_by, changed_at)`, keyed by organization and template, with a foreign key to the template and checks that the JSON names that template and organization; its identity can't change (`catalog_keep_identity`). One current mapping per template, replaced in place with a version counter (an edit says which version it started from); no history. The writer gets INSERT and UPDATE on it (`WRITER_PRIVILEGES`). *Rejected:* versioning mappings like templates (nothing reads an old mapping; public output always uses the current one); putting the mapping inside the template (D-012 keeps them apart, and template history would then change with every mapping edit).
- **Saving** (`PUT /api/templates/:key/dublin-core`, `{ mapping, confirmPublished? }`, `If-None-Match: *` for the first, `If-Match: "v<N>"` after): definitions' writers only (system managers, D-072). The mapping's `template` and `organizationId` are the address's and the session's (another one named is refused); it is checked with core's `validateDublinCoreMapping` against the template's current version **under the template's exclusive definition lock** (taken after the arrangement lock, the order every definition save uses), so a template change can't slip between the check and the write. **Any change** (anything but the identical mapping) counts as showing values at once on every published record using the template, since the mapping decides which public values go out as Dublin Core and JSON-LD: those records are listed and the save needs `confirmPublished` (D-076's gate, `not_permitted` when the person couldn't publish one of them). Reading (`GET`) is for anyone who may read templates; before the first save it is version 0 with `null`.
- **Public reads use the stored mapping.** `readPublicRecord` without `mappings` calls `public.public_dublin_core_mapping(org, site, record)` in the same Repeatable Read, read-only transaction as the snapshot: a SECURITY DEFINER function (search path pinned, names qualified) that returns the record's template's mapping only when the record is public on that site (`record_public_on_site`), else NULL. It joins `PUBLIC_READER_FUNCTIONS`, so `checkPublicReaderConnection` requires the reader to be able to call it and still allows no table at all. A record whose template has no mapping, or whose mapping no longer fits its template (a mapped field made staff only), isn't shown: public output refuses, failing closed (tested). *Rejected:* adding the mapping to `public_record_snapshot` (replacing a large reviewed function for one value); a fallback to the preset mappings (a public field would go out without anyone choosing it).
- **Review fixes (2026-11-24).** A mapping that leaves out the rights for a template whose kinds are published only with them is refused at save (`rights_not_mapped`, public output's own rule), since saving it would take every such published record off the public sites at once. The published records a change shows on are read by ID only under the template's lock (`publishedUsingTemplate`), not the records themselves. The database makes every change the next version (`dublin_core_mappings_next_version`), so the edit's version check can't be passed by direct SQL. No history is kept on purpose (above); who made the current mapping and when are on the row.
- **Left for later:** an admin screen for the mapping (T-050e, with T-048a's template editor) and `npm run setup` storing the starter template's preset mapping (T-050e).
- Sources: as D-012, D-076 and D-080 (DCMI Metadata Terms; PostgreSQL 16 `sql-createfunction.html`, "Writing SECURITY DEFINER Functions Safely").

## D-099 — Desktop follow-ups, part one: `exec.cmd` for Windows, a local password reset with the console as the mailbox, and exit code 4 (2026-11-25, Chat D, S-034a)
- **`exec.cmd`** at the repository root and in `packages/db`: npm runs package scripts in cmd.exe on Windows, which has no `exec`, so `npm run setup`, `db:backup`, `db:restore` and `db:cleanup` didn't even start there. cmd looks in the current folder first, so `exec node …` now runs node through this one-line file (`@%*`); on POSIX the shell's own `exec` runs and D-064's signal forwarding is unchanged (its tests still pin it). **`setup` and `db:cleanup` now work on Windows; `db:backup` and `db:restore` start but still fail there**, because D-064 runs pg_dump and pg_restore under a POSIX `sh` watchdog (S-034, item 11).
- **`npm run desktop:password`** (`packages/api/src/desktop-password-cli.ts`): the ordinary reset (T-039, D-078) with this console as the mailbox. `requestPasswordReset` makes the link for the address typed and hands the token to this process (never shown); `completePasswordReset` sets the password the person types twice. Every rule holds (the password checks, every session ends, two-step sign-in is kept, the reset counters apply, and an account with no second factor in use is refused: only a system admin can reset it, D-078), except that no "your password was changed" email is sent, since the person is at the computer. It uses only the writer address in `desktop.json`, never one from the environment. Whoever can run it can already read the pgpass file and use the database, so it gives no new power. Saying whether an address has an account is fine here for the same reason.
- **Exit code 4** from the launcher: another start is still starting or busy. `start-oculus.cmd` pauses so the message can be read, instead of closing the console at once. (With `npm run desktop` on a Mac or Linux, npm prints its usual note about a non-zero exit.)

**Rejected:** rewriting the npm scripts without `exec` (D-064's signal tests pin `exec`, and schedulers call node directly anyway); a reset that also removes two-step sign-in (that's an admin's reset, T-038, never a self-service one); writing the new hash with SQL directly (it would skip ending sessions, the counters and the password rules).

## D-100 — Public record reads: `GET /records/:id` on a public host, a record's Dublin Core and JSON-LD only, through the public reader with the stored mapping, every refusal the unknown path's 404, braked per address (2026-11-25, T-050d)
Builds T-050d on D-034 (one app), D-016 and S-011c (public output, never `staffWarnings`), D-080 (the public reader), D-090 (hosts) and D-096 (stored mappings). `packages/api/src/public-api.ts` (new), `admin-server.ts`, `rate-limit.ts`. No migration, no new dependency.
- **The route.** `GET /records/:id`, answered only when the request's origin (scheme and host as the request arrives, `X-Forwarded-*` from a trusted proxy only) is one `OCULUS_PUBLIC_SITES` maps to a site, which `checkPublicSites` resolved at startup. The reply is `{ id, dublinCore, jsonLd }` from `readPublicRecord` over the public reader pool, with `baseUrl` `<origin>/records/` and the record's template's stored mapping: three named fields of the result, never the result itself, so `staffWarnings` and anything added to `PublicOutput` later can't leak. It is mounted only when public sites are configured. JSON with the server's usual headers (`no-store`, `nosniff`, the CSP that loads nothing). *Rejected:* `application/ld+json` as the reply type for now (the JSON-LD is one field of the reply; a JSON-LD document of its own can come with the public pages); HTML pages here (Phase 5's site builder renders them from these reads, D-054).
- **One refusal.** On a public host, a malformed or upper-case ID and a record that doesn't exist, isn't published, is another site's or organization's, has no stored mapping, or whose output core refuses all answer `404 not_found`, byte for byte the unknown path's reply there. On any other host (the admin's, an unlisted one, a listed host on another port) it is the same JSON `404 not_found` (on the admin's host an unknown path gets the admin's page instead, so the route's existence shows there; it isn't secret). A method other than GET or HEAD is `405`, as on every route. A fault while reading is `503 unavailable`, logged as a fixed `service_error` with the error's class name only, never its message or the ID.
- **Two brakes.** Per client: 300 reads a minute (`public` in `DEFAULT_RATE_LIMITS`, the in-memory per-minute window of rate-limit.ts), an IPv6 address counted by its /64 (one client usually holds a whole /64, so changing addresses inside it doesn't reset the count). In all: at most `maxConcurrent` reads at once (4, the public pool's size); past that a read is refused at once with `503 busy`, so requests never queue for a connection behind slow reads (review: a few clients under their own limits could otherwise fill the pool and grow an unbounded queue). A `statement_timeout` and a connection wait limit on the reader pool itself are S-037. *Rejected:* a cache now (published output changes on publish, withdraw, template and mapping saves; correct invalidation is its own task).
- **For T-051's runbook:** behind a proxy, `OCULUS_TRUSTED_PROXY` must name it, or every visitor shares one address's 300 reads a minute (and an https origin isn't matched at all, D-090). A 429 carries no `Retry-After` (the error path drops handler headers).
- **Tests:** a published record's reply holds its URL, Dublin Core and JSON-LD and no staff value or `staffWarnings`; on the public host a record without a stored mapping, a draft, another site's and another organization's records, a made-up, a malformed and an upper-case ID give the unknown path's exact body; the admin's host, an unlisted host and the public host on another port give 404; a POST is 405; the brake answers 429 past the limit and resets after the minute; reads past the concurrent limit get `503 busy` and the slot is given back; IPv6 keys by /64.
- Sources: as D-016, D-080 and D-090; OWASP ASVS 5.0 V8 (answer what may not be seen as if it didn't exist).

## D-102 — Backups on Windows (tools run directly, found in the installer's folders) and a backup before every desktop update (2026-11-25, Chat D, S-034b)
D-064 runs pg_dump and pg_restore under a POSIX `sh` watchdog in their own process group, so backups and restores failed on Windows (D-099's note).

- **Two ways to run a tool** (`ToolRunner` in backup.ts): `watchdog` (D-064, unchanged, the default everywhere but Windows) and `direct` (the default on Windows): the tool is spawned with no shell, its input is its stdin, stopping it terminates it (an unfinished pg_restore's one transaction never commits, so it rolls back), and it's registered for the exit clean-up by its PID (`trackProcess`). What `direct` gives up: if this process is killed outright, the tool may run on until it ends by itself; its server session then finishes or rolls back, as for a lost connection. `backup` and `restore` take `toolRunner` so the tests run the `direct` path on Linux too (round trip, stop mid-backup, stop mid-restore).
- **Finding the tools on Windows:** `.exe` names, and when `PG_BIN` isn't set and `pg_config` isn't on PATH (the EDB installer adds neither), `%ProgramFiles%\\PostgreSQL\\<major>\\bin`, newest major first. `SystemRoot` is passed to `--version`'s minimal environment, which Windows programs need to start.
- **A backup before every desktop update:** when the code has migrations the database hasn't, the launcher backs up (as the owner) into the settings folder's `backups` (0700) before migrating, and if the backup fails, migrates nothing and says why. `"backupBeforeUpdate": false` in `desktop.json` turns it off.

**Rejected:** PowerShell or a job object as a Windows watchdog (more moving parts than the risk warrants for one person's desktop; a job object needs native code); asking before each update instead of backing up (the person would usually just press Enter); skipping the backup when the tools are missing (an update could then lose data with no way back).

**Sources:** Node.js `child_process` documentation (`spawn`, `subprocess.kill` on Windows: TerminateProcess, signals not delivered); EDB's Windows installer layout (`C:\\Program Files\\PostgreSQL\\<major>\\bin`).


## D-105 — OAI-PMH 2.0 on public hosts: `GET /oai`, oai_dc only, every record read again through the public reader, `text/xml` only through `oaiPmhReply` (2026-11-25, Chat C, T-050c)

Builds T-050c on D-100 (public record reads), D-095 (lists on the staff connection), D-096 (stored mappings) and D-058/S-025 (XML is active content).

- **The route.** `GET /oai` on a host `OCULUS_PUBLIC_SITES` maps to a site answers the six verbs with `oai_dc` only (`packages/api/src/oai-pmh.ts`); on the admin's or an unlisted host it is the unknown path's 404. It is mounted by `buildAdminServer` only when `OCULUS_OAI_ADMIN_EMAIL` gives Identify's required `adminEmail` (one plain address; anything else stops the start). POST isn't offered: rule 5 needs an `Origin` on every change, and harvesters send GET.
- **What is shown.** The lists come from the staff connection (`listPublishedForOai` in `packages/db/src/oai.ts`: records public on the site, oldest datestamp first, ties by ID), and every listed record is read again through `readPublicRecord` on the public reader, as `GET /records/:id` reads it. A record public output holds back is left out: neither its metadata nor its identifier leaves. GetRecord and ListMetadataFormats with an identifier read the record through the public reader first and answer `idDoesNotExist` for anything it refuses. Its metadata is core's `oaiDcXml` with its XML declaration dropped.
- **Identifiers and datestamps.** Identifiers are `oai:<host>:<record ID>` (the oai-identifier form). A datestamp is the later of the record's current version (`record_versions.recorded_at`: every edit and workflow move writes one) and its template's stored mapping's `changed_at`, to the second (`YYYY-MM-DDThh:mm:ssZ`); `from` and `until` take either granularity (the same in both), `until` inclusive. **Limit:** a change to another record its output shows (a parent's title, a linked record) or to a vocabulary label doesn't move the datestamp, so incremental harvests miss it until the record itself changes; a full harvest picks it up. `earliestDatestamp` is the site's earliest, or 1970-01-01 with no records. `deletedRecord` is `no`: unpublishing simply removes a record from the lists. No sets (`noSetHierarchy`).
- **Paging.** 25 records (or headers) a page; a stateless resumption token (base64url of the window and the last record's exact datestamp and ID, refused unless canonical) resumes after that record, so a record that changes mid-harvest comes again later and nothing is skipped. A request reads at most 200 listed records; the last page of a resumed list ends with an empty `<resumptionToken/>`. A page whose every listed record is held back can come back empty with a token (schema-invalid, functional; rare).
- **Serving `text/xml`.** `oaiPmhReply` (file-replies.ts, under server rule 11) is the only way out: the document must start with the XML declaration and the OAI-PMH root in its namespace, hold no other `<?` (no `xml-stylesheet`) and no `<!` (no DOCTYPE, entity, CDATA or comment), end with `</OAI-PMH>`, and be at most 16 MiB. It is sent inline as `text/xml; charset=utf-8` with the file replies' headers (nosniff, the sandboxing CSP that loads nothing, same-origin CORP) and nothing the handler set. Every value written is escaped; argument values XML 1.0 can't hold are `badArgument`, and stored names lose such characters. `badVerb` and `badArgument` replies echo no arguments.
- **Brakes.** Each client address (IPv6 by /64) may make 12 OAI-PMH requests a minute (`oai` in `DEFAULT_RATE_LIMITS`), then `429` with `Retry-After: 60`. A request holds one slot of the public pool for its whole page; the slots (`ReadSlots`, 4) are shared with `GET /records/:id`, and with none free the answer is `503 busy` with `Retry-After: 5` at once. Faults are `503 unavailable`, logged by error class name only.
- **Rejected:** a public-reader listing function (D-095's review: it would hand the reader the IDs of held-back records); `deletedRecord` `transient` or `persistent` (needs a record of unpublishing nothing keeps yet); sets by collection or record type (later, if harvesters ask); serving through `type()` (S-025 withdrew that).
- **Tests** (`packages/api/src/oai-pmh.test.ts`): Identify, formats, GetRecord and both lists for the public records only (draft, held-back, other site's and other organization's records absent by ID too), paging each record once to an empty token, `from`/`until`, every error code and when arguments are echoed, escaping, the hosts, mounting by the admin email, the brakes, and `oaiPmhReply` refusing HTML, SVG, a stylesheet instruction, DOCTYPE, CDATA, comments and trailing content.
- Sources: The Open Archives Initiative Protocol for Metadata Harvesting, version 2.0 (§3.3 datestamps and granularity, §3.5 flow control and resumption tokens, §3.6 errors, §4 verbs), https://www.openarchives.org/OAI/openarchivesprotocol.html, and its oai-identifier guidelines; W3C XML 1.0 (Fifth Edition) §2.2 characters; as D-058 and D-100.
